8.4 Using NAT Gateway for Internet Access

  What is a a NAT Gateway
    - you can use Network Address Translation (NAT) gateway to enable instances
      in private subnets to connect to the internet or other AWS services while
      preventing the internet from initiating a connection with those instances

  Network Diagram
     - NAT gateway is provisioned in the public subnet
     - NAT gateway acts as means for internet traffic to go out


     VPC (acloudguruVPC) - 10.0.0.0/16
        [public subnet  (10.0.1.0/24) ec2 security group NAT Gateway]  <-|                | Route table <-|
                                                                         |- Network ACL <-|               |- router <- internet gateway
        [private subnet (10.0.2.0/24) ec2 security group            ]  <-|                | Route table <-|

  NAT Gateway Tips
    5 NAT Gateway Facts
       - redundant inside AZ
       - Starts at 5 Gbps and scales currently to 45 Gbps
       - no need to patch NAT gateways
       - not associated with Security Groups
       - automatically assigned a Public IP address
       - must disable source/destination check on NAT instance

  Console Demo

    -> NAT Gateway -> Create NAT Gateway -> Name: my-nat-gateway, subnet: (public) 10.0.1.0/24 - us-east-1a, allocate Elastic IP
        -> create

     # need to add route in route table for NAT Gateway
     -> Route Table -> select acloudguruVPC main route (now named: acloudguruRoute) -> Routes ->
        -> edit routes -> add route -> Destination: 0.0.0.0/0 target: NAT Gateway -> save changes


  Exam Tips for NAT Gateways
    5 NAT Gateway Facts
       - redundant inside AZ
       - Starts at 5 Gbps and scales currently to 45 Gbps
       - no need to patch NAT gateways
       - not associated with Security Groups
       - automatically assigned a Public IP address
       - NAT Gateway (NGW) allows instances with no public IPs to access the internet.
       - NAT Gateway does something similar to Internet Gateway (IGW), but it only works one way: Instances
         in a private subnet can connect to services outside your VPC but external services cannot initiate
         a connection with those instances.
       - You are charged for each hour that your NAT gateway is available and each Gigabyte of data that it processes.
       - must disable source/destination check on NAT instance


---------------------------------------------------
8.5 Protecting your Resources with Security Groups

  Review of Security Groups
   How Computers Communicate
      - communicate over certain protocols which use ports
      Linux: ssh (port 22)
      Windows: RDP (port 3389) -> when use "remote desktop protocol" to remotely access your windows machine
      HTTP : Web Browsing (Port 80)
      HTTPS : Encrypted Web Browsing (SSL) (Port 443)


  Network Diagram
     VPC (acloudguruVPC) - 10.0.0.0/16
        [public subnet  (10.0.1.0/24) ec2 security group NAT Gateway]  <-|                | Route table <-|
                                                                         |- Network ACL <-|               |- router <- internet gateway
        [private subnet (10.0.2.0/24) ec2 security group            ]  <-|                | Route table <-|

     - if a connectity issue there is with your ec2:
        - check if route table has a route
        - check Network ACLS
        - check Security Group

   Security Groups
     - virtual firewalls for your EC2 instances
     - By default, everything is blocked
     - to open a port to everything, use: 0.0.0.0/0  (in production env, not only should be used for 80 and 443)
     - in order to communicate to your ec2 instance via ssh, rdp, http, etc, you need to open up the correct ports

   Security Groups are stateful
     - security groups are stateful - if you send a request from your instance, the response
       traffic for that request is allowed to flow in regardless of inbound security group rules
     - responses to allow inbound traffic are allow to flow out, regardless of outbound rules

  Exam Tips

---------------------------------------------------
8.6 Controlling Subnet Traffic with Network ACLs

  Ephermeral Ports
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc/latest/userguide/vpc-network-acls.html#nacl-ephemeral-ports
  Wikipedia Ephemeral ports
  https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ephemeral_port

  What i`s a Network ACL
    - an optional layer of security for your VPC that acts as a firewall for
      controlloing traffic in and out of one or more subnets
    - you might set up network ACLs with rules similar to your Security Groups
      in order to add another layer of security to your VPC

  Network Diagram
     valid IP address ranges:
        10.0.0.0    - 10.255.255.255  (10/8 prefix) - most companies use
        172.16.0.0  - 172.31.255.255  (172.16/12 prefix)
        192.168.0.0 - 192.168.255.255 (192.168/16 prefix)

     VPC (acloudguruVPC) - 10.0.0.0/16
        [public subnet  (10.0.1.0/24) ec2 security group NAT Gateway]  <-|                | Route table <-|
                                                                         |- Network ACL <-|               |- router |<- internet gateway
        [private subnet (10.0.2.0/24) ec2 security group            ]  <-|                | Route table <-|         |
                                                                                                                    |<-Virtual Private
  Network ACLs Overview
    Default Network ACLs
      - Your VPC automatically comes with a default network ACL, and by default it allows all outbound
        and inbound traffic
    Custom Network ACLs
      - You can create custom network ACLs. By default, each custom network ACL denies all inbound and
        outbound traffic until you add rules
    Subnet Associations:
      - Each subnet in your VPC must be associated with a network ACL
      - If you don't explicitly associate a subnet with a network ACL, the subnet is automatically
        associated with the default network ACL
    Block IP Addresses
      - Block IP addressing using ACLs, not security Groups

  Network ACLs Tips
    Network ACL subnet association
      - you can associate a network ACL with multiple subnets
      - however, a subnet can be associated with only 1 network ACL at a time
      - when you associate a network ACL with a subnet, the previous associate is removed
    Network ACL rules
      - Network ACLs contained a number list of rules that are evaluated in order, starting with
        the lowest numbered rule
      - Network ACLs have separate inbound and outbound rules, each rule can either
        allow or deny traffic
    Network ACLs are stateless
      - responses to allowed inbound traffic are subject to the rules for outbound traffic
        and vice versa

  Console Demo
     -> ec2 webserver, install apache and start apache
       $ sudo yum install httpd -y
       $ sudo service httpd start
       $ cd /var/www/html
       $ sudo su
       $ echo "<html><body><h1>Hello Cloud Gurus</h1></body></html>" > index.html

       -> with web browser, verify <webServer public IP> displays "Hello Cloud Gurus"

     -> VPC -> Network ACL -> examine acloudguruVPC default VPC
        -> allows allow inbound and outbound traffic

     -> Create Network ACL -> Name: MyWebNACL, vpc: acloudguruVPC -> create
        Note: this associate "MyWebNACL" with acloudguruVPC, but it does not change the default acloudguruVPC Network ACL
              That is, this new VPC is simply now available to acloudguru VPC as a NACL that can be associated with its subnets

      -> select "MyWebNACL" -> edit subnet association -> select "10.0.1.0/24 - us-east-1a" -> save

      -> with web browser, verify <webServer public IP> is not longer reachable due to MyWebNACL

      Note: AWS recommends start rule with rule number 100 and increment by 100
      -> MyWebNACL -> inbound rules -> edit
        -> add rule: 100  type: HTTP(80) source: 0.0.0.0/0 allow
        -> add rule: 200  type: SSH(22)  source: 0.0.0.0/0 allow
        -> save
      -> MyWebNACL -> outbound rules -> edit
        -> add rule: 100  type: HTTP(80) source: 0.0.0.0/0 allow
        -> add rule: 200  type: SSH(22)  source: 0.0.0.0/0 allow
        #  rule to allow ephemeral ports outbound traffic
        -> add rule: 300  type: custom TCP  port range: 1024-65535  source: 0.0.0.0/0 allow
        -> save

     Ephemeral ports:
        - when a inbound requests comes on a ports such HTTP (80), it choose a random ephemeral port to
          reply on, so the potential ephemeral ports must be open to outbound traffic

       -> with web browser, verify <webServer public IP> displays "Hello Cloud Gurus"

  Network ACL Exam Tips

    Default Network ACLs
      - Your VPC automatically comes with a default network ACL, and by default it allows all outbound
        and inbound traffic
    Custom Network ACLs
      - You can create custom network ACLs. By default, each custom network ACL denies all inbound and
        outbound traffic until you add rules
    Subnet Associations:
      - Each subnet in your VPC must be associated with a network ACL
      - If you don't explicitly associate a subnet with a network ACL, the subnet is automatically
        associated with the default network ACL
    Block IP Addresses
      - Block IP addressing using ACLs, not security Groups

    Network ACL subnet association
      - you can associate a network ACL with multiple subnets
      - however, a subnet can be associated with only 1 network ACL at a time
      - when you associate a network ACL with a subnet, the previous associatation is removed
    Network ACL rules
      - Network ACLs contained a number list of rules that are evaluated in order, starting with
        the lowest numbered rule
      - Network ACLs have separate inbound and outbound rules, each rule can either
        allow or deny traffic
    Network ACLs are stateless
      - responses to allowed inbound traffic are subject to the rules for outbound traffic
        and vice versa

8.7 Private Communication Using VPC Endpoints

  What are VPC Endpoints
    - enables you to privately connect your VPC to supported AWS services and VPC endpoint
      services powered by PrivateLink without requiring an internet gateway, NAT gateway, VPN
      connection, or AWS Direct Connect connection
    - instances in your VPC do not require public IP addresses to comunicate with resources in
      the service
    - traffic between your VPC and other services does not leave the Amazon network

  Endpoint Are Virtual Devices
    - they are horizontally scaled, redundant, and highly available VPC components that
      allow communication between instances in your VPC and services without imposing
      availability risks or bandwidth contraints on you network traffic

  VPC Endpoint 2 Types
    Interface Endpoints
      - an elastic network interface with a private IP address that serves as an entry point
        for traffic headed to a supported service.
      - they support a large number of AWS services
    Gateway Endpoints
      - Similar to NAT Gateway, a gateway endpoint is a virtual device you provision
      - It supports connection to S3 and DynamoDB

  VPC Endpoint policies:
    - When you create an interface endpoint or a gateway endpoint, you can attach an endpoint policy. The endpoint
      policy is a JSON policy document that controls which AWS principals can use the VPC endpoint to access the endpoint service.
    - You cannot attach more than one policy to an endpoint. However, you can modify an endpoint policy at any time.
    - An endpoint policy does not override or replace identity-based policies or resource-based policies


  VPC Endpoints in Action

  Console Demo
    -> attach "S3_ADMIN_ACCESS_FOR_EC2" role to "MyDBServer" ec2 instance
    -> goto VPC -> Endpoints -> Create Endpoint -> Name: S3EP, Services: S3 gateway (com.amazonwwas.us-east-1.s3),
       VPC: acloudguruVPC, route table: (default used by private subnet)

     from MyDBServer login window:
     $ aws s3 ls

  Exam Tips
     VPC Endpoints
       Use case: when you want to connect to AWS services without leaving the Amazon internal network
       2 types of endpoints: Interface endpoints and gateway endpoints
       Gateway Endpoints: support S3 and DynamoDB

---------------------------------------------------
8.8 Building Solutions across VPC with Peering

  Update your route tables for a VPC peering connection
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc/latest/peering/vpc-peering-routing.html

  Multiple VPCs
    - Sometimes you may need to have several VPCs for different environments, and it may be
      necessary to connect these VPCs to each other

    examples: Separate VPCs for different use cases
       production Web VPC
       content VPC
       intranet VPC

  VPC Peering
    - allows you to connect 1 VPC with another via a direct network route using private IP addresses
    - instances behave as if they were on the same private network
    - you can peer VPCs with other AWS accounts as well as with other VPCs in the same account
    - peering is in a star configuration (e.g. 1 central VPC peers with 4 others)
    - NO transitive peering (VPC peering must be directly between the VPCs, you canNOT go through another VPC)
    - you can peer between regions

  Transitive Peering
    - VPC peering must be directly between the VPCs, you canNOT go through another VPC (e.g. no transitive peering)

  Console Demo

     -> goto VPC -> Peering Connections -> Create Peering Connection -> Name: MyPeer,
        Local VPC to peer with: acloudguruVPC,  Select another VPC to peer with: default VPC -> Create
        -> goto ACTION -> accept connection

        -> each Route table add: target: Peering connection -> select, destination: 172.31.0.0/16 or 10.0.0.0/16

  Exam Tips
    VPC Peering
      - allows you to connect 1 VPC with another via direct network route using private IP address
      - transitive peering is not supported - The 2 VPCs must have a direct VPC peering connection
      - you can peer between regions
      - you can not have overlapping CIDR address ranges
      - must include Peering Connection in route tables

---------------------------------------------------
8.9 Network Privacy with AWS PrivateLink

  Opening Your Services in a VPC to another VPC
    1. Open the VPCs up the internet
      - Open both VPCs to public access, and share across public access
      - security considerations; everything is in the public subnet is public
      - a lot more to manage (multiple gateways, route tables, ...)
    2. Use VPC Peering
      - You will have to create an manage many different peering relationships
      - the whole network will be accessible. This isn't good if you have multiple applications
        within your VPC
     3. Using Private Link

  Using PrivateLink
    - the best way to expose a service VPC to tens, hundreds, or thousands of customer VPCs
    - doesn't require VPC Peering; no route tables, NAT Gateways, internet gateways, etc.
    - requires a Network Load Balancer on the service VPC and ENI (Elastic Network Interface)
      on the customer VPC

      Service VPC                  AWS PrivateLink                  Customer VPC
      |Application     Network Load  |         | Elastic Network     Application |
      | service         Balancer     |         | Interface (ENI)     Service     |

  Exam Tips

    AWS PrivateLink
    - the best way to expose a service VPC to tens, hundreds, or thousands of customer VPCs
    - doesn't require VPC Peering; no route tables, NAT Gateways, internet gateways, etc.
    - requires a Network Load Balancer on the service VPC and ENI (Elastic Network Interface)
      on the customer VPC

8.10 Securing your Network with VPN CloudHub

  VPN CloudHub
    - If you have multiple sites, each with its own VPN connection, you can use
      AWS VPN CloudHub to connect those sites together
    - Hub-and-spoke model
    - low cost and easy to manage
    - It operates over the public internet, but all traffic between customer gateway
      and AWS VPN CloudHub is encrypted

  Network Diagram
     Enables customer site 1 to talk with customer site 2 via their VPN connections to AWS


  Exam Tips
  AWS VPN CloudHub
    - If you have multiple sites, each with its own VPN connection, you can use
      AWS VPN CloudHub to connect those sites together
    - It is similar to VPC peering in that it works on a hub-and-spoke model
    - low cost and easy to manage
    - It operates over the public internet, but all traffic between customer gateway
      and AWS VPN CloudHub is encrypted

8.11 Connecting On-Premises with Direct Connect (DX)

  Direct Connect
    - is a cloud service solution that makes it easy to establish a dedicated network
      connection from your premises to AWS

  Private Connectivity
    - Using AWS Direct Connect, you can establish private connectivity between AWS and
      your data center or office
    - in many cases, you can reduce your network cost, increase bandwidth throughput, and
      provide a more consistent network experience than internet-based connection

  2 Types of Connections
    Dedicated Connection
      - a physical ethernet connnection associated with single customer
      - customers can request a dedicated connection through the AWS Direct Connect Console,
        CLI, or API
    Hosted Connection
      - a physical ethernet connnection that an AWS Direct Connect Partner provisions on behalf
        of a customer
      - Customer request a hosted connection by contacting a partner in the AWS Direct Connect
        Partner Program who provisions the connection

  Direct Connect in Action
       At an AWS Direct Connect (DX) location
           either:                  direct connect cage side-by-side with customer cage
        AWS Region <-> DX Connetion  <->  DX Router <->  customer router <-> customer link to on-premise

           or:                     direct connect cage side-by-side with customer partner cage
        AWS Region <-> DX Connetion  <->  DX Router <->  cust/partner router <-> customer link to on-premise

  VPNs vs Direct Connect
    VPNs
      - VPNs allow private communication, but it still traverse the public internet to get data
        delivered. While secure it can be slow
    Direct Connect:
      - Fast, secure, reliable, and able to take massive throughput

  Exam Tips
    Direct Conect (DX)
      - Direct Connect directly connects your data center to AWS
      - Useful for high-througphput workloads (e.g. lots of network traffic)
      - Helpful when you need a stable and reliable secure connection

---------------------------------------------------
8.12 Simplifying Networks with Transit Gateway (TGW)

  Network Architecture Diagram
    - can get complicated when you have many VPC with peering between the VPCs pluse on-premise connections

  Transit Gateway (TGW)
    - Connects VPCs and on-premise networks through a central hub
    - This simplifies your network and puts an end to the complex peering relationships
    - It acts as a cloud router - each new connection is only made once

  Simplify Your Network Topology
    - connects all VPC and on-premise connections to the transit gateway, then they all call communicate

  Transit Gateway Facts
    - allows you to have a transitve peering between thousands of VPCs and on-premise data centers
    - works on a hub-and-spoke model
    - works on a regional basis, but you can have it across multiple regions
    - You can use it across multiple AWS accounts using RAM (Resource Access Manager)

  Exam Tips
    Transit Gateway
      - you can use route tables to limit how VPCs talk to one another
      - works with Direct Connect and well as VPN connections
      - Supports IP  multicast (not supported by any other AWS Service)
           IP multicast: used to send a single audio/video to thousand of users

  AWS PrivateLink and comparison to Transit Gateway / VPC Peering
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/whitepapers/latest/building-scalable-secure-multi-vpc-network-infrastructure/aws-privatelink.html

  AWS Private Link vs Transit Gateway:
    AWS PrivateLink
     - Use AWS PrivateLink when you have a client/server set up where you want to allow one or more consumer
       VPCs unidirectional access to a specific service or set of instances in the service provider VPC.
     - Only the clients in the consumer VPC can initiate a connection to the service in the service provider VPC.
     - This is also a good option when client and servers in the two VPCs have overlapping IP addresses as
       AWS PrivateLink uses ENIs within the client VPC in a manner that ensures that are no IP conflicts with
       the service provider.
     - You can access AWS PrivateLink endpoints over VPC Peering, VPN, and AWS Direct Connect.

    VPC peering and Transit Gateway
     - Use VPC peering and Transit Gateway when you want to enable layer-3 IP connectivity between VPCs.

     - Your architecture will contain a mix of these technologies in order to fulfill different use cases.
     - All of these services can be combined and operated with each other. For example, AWS PrivateLink
       handling API style client-server connectivity, VPC peering for handling direct connectivity requirements
       where placement groups may still be desired within the Region or inter-Region connectivity is needed,
       and Transit Gateway to simplify connectivity of VPCs at scale as well as edge consolidation for hybrid connectivity.

  Transit Gateway Question:
   Q: The network will be highly complex with over 1,000 VPCs (all of which will need to communicate to each other)
      as well as having transitive peering between some on-premises data centers while all supporting IP Multicast.
      What VPC Network Solution should you recommend?
   A: AWS Transit Gateway connects your VPCs and on-premises networks through a central hub. This simplifies your
      network and puts an end to complex peering relationships. It acts as a cloud router; each new connection
      is only made once.

8.13 5G Networking with AWS Wavelength

  5G Networks
    - 5G provides mobile devices with higher speed, lower latency, and greater
      capacity than 4G LTE networks.

  AWS Wavelength
    - embeds AWS compute and storage services within 5G networks, providing mobile edge
      computing infrastructure for developing, deploying, and scaling ultra-low-latency
      applications

  Exam Tips
     AWS Wavelength
       - if you see a scenario question about 5G increasing application speeds at the edge
         using mobile network, think AWS Wavelength

8.14 VPC Networking Exam Tips

  VPCs
    - VP is a virtual data center in the cloud
    - Consists of internet gateways (or virtual private gateways), route tables,
      network access control lists, subnets, and security groups
    - 1 subnet is always in 1 AZ

  NAT Gateway
    - you can use Network Address Translation (NAT) gateway to enable instances
      in private subnets to connect to the internet or other AWS services while
      preventing the internet from initiating a connection with those instances
    - NAT Gateway (NGW) allows instances with no public IPs to access the internet.
    - NAT Gateway does something similar to Internet Gateway (IGW), but it only works one way: Instances
      in a private subnet can connect to services outside your VPC but external services cannot initiate
      a connection with those instances.
    - You are charged for each hour that your NAT gateway is available and each Gigabyte of data that it processes.

  5 NAT Gateway Facts
       - redundant inside AZ
       - Starts at 5 Gbps and scales currently to 45 Gbps
       - no need to patch NAT gateways
       - not associated with Security Groups
       - automatically assigned a Public IP address
       - must disable source/destination check on NAT instance

   High Availability with NAT Gateways
     - if you have resources in multiple AZs and they share a NAT gateway, in the event the NAT gateway's
       AZ is down, the resources in the other AZs lose internet access
     - To create an AZ-independent architecture, create a NAT gateway in eachc AZ and configure your
       routing to ensure resources use the NAT gateway in the same AZ

   Security Groups are stateful
     - security groups are stateful - if you send a request from your instance, the response
       traffic for that request is allowed to flow in regardless of inbound security group rules
     - responses to allow inbound traffic are allow to flow out, regardless of outbound rules

   Security Groups
     - virtual firewalls for your EC2 instances
     - By default, everything is blocked
     - to open a port to everything, use: 0.0.0.0/0  (in production env, not only should be used for 80 and 443)
     - in order to communicate to your ec2 instance via ssh, rdp, http, etc, you need to open up the correct ports

  Network ACL Exam Tips

    Default Network ACLs
      - Your VPC automatically comes with a default network ACL, and by default it allows all outbound
        and inbound traffic
    Custom Network ACLs
      - You can create custom network ACLs. By default, each custom network ACL denies all inbound and
        outbound traffic until you add rules
    Subnet Associations:
      - Each subnet in your VPC must be associated with a network ACL
      - If you don't explicitly associate a subnet with a network ACL, the subnet is automatically
        associated with the default network ACL
    Block IP Addresses
      - Block IP addressing using ACLs, not security Groups

    Network ACL subnet association
      - you can associate a network ACL with multiple subnets
      - however, a subnet can be associated with only 1 network ACL at a time
      - when you associate a network ACL with a subnet, the previous associatation is removed
    Network ACL rules
      - Network ACLs contained a number list of rules that are evaluated in order, starting with
        the lowest numbered rule
      - Network ACLs have separate inbound and outbound rules, each rule can either
        allow or deny traffic
    Network ACLs are stateless
      - responses to allowed inbound traffic are subject to the rules for outbound traffic
        and vice versa
      - you may need to have ephemeral ports (1024 - 65535) to opened in for outbound traffic
        initiated as responses to inbound traffic

  Direct Connect (DX)
      - Direct Connect directly connects your data center to AWS
      - Useful for high-througphput workloads (e.g. lots of network traffic)
      - Helpful when you need a stable and reliable secure connection

  Direct Connect Gateway
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/directconnect/latest/UserGuide/direct-connect-gateways-intro.html
    Use AWS Direct Connect gateway to connect your VPCs. You associate an AWS Direct Connect gateway with
    either of the following gateways:
     - A transit gateway when you have multiple VPCs in the same Region
     - A virtual private gateway

       Customer                 Private       Direct  |<-- VPG Assoc -> VPC1 *  **
       op-premise <---- DX ---> virtual  <--> Connect |
       Network                  Interfae      Gateway |<-- VPG Assoc -> VPC2 *  **

       Notes: * VPC1 and VPC2 are in different Regions
              ** Each VPC has a virtual private gateway that connects to the Direct Connect gateway
                 using a virtual private gateway association

       Note: Direct Connect Gateway can be used connect multiple accounts and on-premise

  VPC Endpoints
       Use case: when you want to connect to AWS services without leaving the Amazon internal network
       2 types of endpoints: Interface endpoints and gateway endpoints
       Gateway Endpoints: support S3 and DynamoDB and do not use AWS PrivateLink
       Interface endpoints: enable connectivity to AWS servoces over AWS Privatelink

  VPC Peering
      - allows you to connect 1 VPC with another via direct network route using private IP address
      - transitive peering is not supported - The 2 VPCs must have a direct VPC peering connection
      - instances behave as if they were on the same private network
      - you can peer VPCs with other AWS accounts as well as with other VPCs in same account
      - you can peer between regions
      - you can not have overlapping CIDR address ranges
      - must include Peering Connection in route tables

  AWS PrivateLink
    - the best way to expose a service VPC to tens, hundreds, or thousands of customer VPCs
    - doesn't require VPC Peering; no route tables, NAT Gateways, internet gateways, etc.
    - requires a Network Load Balancer on the service VPC and ENI (Elastic Network Interface)
      on the customer VPC

  Transit Gateway
      - you can use route tables to limit how VPCs talk to one another
      - works with Direct Connect and well as VPN connections
      - Supports IP  multicast (not supported by any other AWS Service)
           IP multicast: used to send a single audio/video to thousand of users
      - AWS Transit Gateway is a fully managed service that connects VPCs and On-Premises networks
        through a central hub without relying on numerous point-to-point connections or Transit VPC.

  AWS VPN CloudHub
    - If you have multiple sites, each with its own VPN connection, you can use
      AWS VPN CloudHub to connect those sites together
    - It is similar to VPC peering in that it works on a hub-and-spoke model
    - low cost and easy to manage
    - It operates over the public internet, but all traffic between customer gateway
      and AWS VPN CloudHub is encrypted

      Cloudhub diagram:

       |--------|         |<----- VPN ------> Customer Gateway ASN:6500 <-> customer network New York
       |  AWS   | Virtual |
       |  VPC   | private |<----- VPN ------> Customer Gateway ASN:6501 <-> customer network LA
       |        | Gateway |
       |--------|         |<----- VPN ------> Customer Gateway ASN:6502 <-> customer network Miami

       Notes: Each Customer Gateway must have public IP address of the gateway
              You must use a unique Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) Autonomous System Number (ASN) for each customer gateway.
              BPG AS stands for Autonomous System

  AWS Wavelength
       - if you see a scenario question about 5G increasing application speeds at the edge
         using mobile network, think AWS Wavelength

Question 2: What is the IPv4 CIDR block of the default VPC?

   10.0.0.0/16
   192.168.1.0/16
   172.31.0.0/16
   172.0.0.0/16

Chapter 9: Route 53

  [Route 53] Supported DNS Record types:
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/ResourceRecordTypes.html

    A (address) record:
     - contains IPv4 address for where to route traffic to

    AAAA (IPv6 address) record:
     - contains IPv6 address for where to route traffic to

    CAA (Certificatte authorities) record
    - specifies which certificate authoritties (CAs) allowed to issue certificates for a domain or subdomain

    CNAME (canonical name) record:
     -  A CNAME record maps DNS queries for the name of the current record, such as acme.example.com
        or www.example.com, to another domain (example.com or example.net) or subdomain (acme.example.com
        or zenith.example.org).
      - cannot use CNAME to map zone apex (naked domaini, e.g. example.com) to another domain or subdomain. You are
        suppose use A and AAAA record to map zone apex names to IP addresses.  However, sometimes you
        want to map zone apex name to a non-static IP address (e.g. ELB, S3 bucket, etc.). AWS solution
        is alias records

      - example CNAME record:
        domainname          TTL                    domain to map to
        www.example.com.    3600    IN    CNAME    webALB-1234567890.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com

    DS (delegation signer) Record Type
     - A delegation signer (DS) record refers a zone key for a delegated subdomain zone. You might
       create a DS record when you establish a chain of trust when you configure DNSSEC signing.

    MX (mail server) record
     - An MX record specifies the names of your email servers and, if you have two or more email servers,
       the priority order.
     - With an MX record, it's possible to hand off emails to a dedicated email server.

     NAPTR record type
      - A Name Authority Pointer (NAPTR) is a type of record that is used by Dynamic Delegation Discovery
        System (DDDS) applications to convert one value to another or to replace one value with another.
        For example, one common use is to convert phone numbers into SIP URIs.

     NS record type
       - An NS record identifies the name servers for the hosted zone. Note the following:
         - The most common use for an NS record is to control how internet traffic is routed for a domain.
       - Nameservers connect your domain name to the actual server your site is hosted on. The nameserver contains
         other DNS records for the domain like an A record and MX record.

     PTR (Pointer) record type
       - A PTR record maps an IP address to the corresponding domain name.
       - PTR record provides a domain name for reverse lookup. It's the opposite of an A record as it provides
         the domain name linked to an IP address instead of the IP address for a domain.

     SOA record type
      - A start of authority (SOA) record provides information about a domain and the corresponding Amazon
        Route 53 hosted zone.
      - SOA stores admin information about a domain. This information includes the email address of the admin
        and when the domain was last updated.

---------------------------------------------------
9.1 Route 53 Overview
  working with private hosted zones
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/hosted-zones-private.html

  working with public hosted zones
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/AboutHZWorkingWith.html

  Route 53 FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/route53/faqs/

  Route 53 Quotas
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/DNSLimitations.html

  List of Internet top-level domains
    https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Internet_top-level_domains

  List of Internet second-level domains
    https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second-level_domain


  What is DNS (Domain Name System)
    - DNS is used to convert human-friendly domain names (e.g. http://acloud.guru) into
      internet protocol (IP) address (e.g. http://82.124.53.1)
    - IP addresses are used by computers to identify each other on the network
    - IP addresses commonly come in 2 different forms: IPv4 and IPv6

  IPv4 vs IPv6
    IPv4
       - the IPv4 space is 32-bit field and has over 4 billion different addresses
    IPv6
       - create to solve this depletion issue and has address space of 128 bits
       - in theory, there are 340 undecillion addresses
    Route 53 supports both IPv4 and IPv6 addresses

  Top-level Domains
     - if you look at common domain names (e.g. google.com, bbc.co.uk, acloud.guru),
       you will notice a string of characters separated by dots
     - the last word in a domain name represents to the top-level domain (e.g com, uk, guru)
     - the second word in a domain is known as a second-level domain (this is optional,
       though, and depends on the domain name) (e.g. uk)
     - common top-level domain names: .gov  .gov.uk   .com.au  .edu
     - top-level domain names are controlled by the Internet Assigned Numbers Authority (IANA)
       in a root zone database which is essentially a database of all available top-level domains

     - IANA database is:
       http://www.iana.org/domains/root/db

  Domain Registrars
    - because all names in a give domain name must be unique, there needs to be a way to organize
      this all so that domain names are not duplicated
    - A registrar is an authority that can assign domain names directly under one or more top-level
      domains. These are domains are registered with InterNIC, a service of ICANN (Internet Corporation
      for Assigned Names and Numbers), which enfores uniqueness of domain names across the internet
    - Each domain name becomes registered in a central database known as the WHOIS database

  5 Popular Domain Registrars
    - domain.com
    - GoDaddy
    - Hover
    - AWS
    - Namecheap


  Common DNS Record Types
    SOA (Start of Authority):
      - SOA record store information about
         - the name of the server that supplied the data for the zone
         - the administrator of the zone
         - the current version of the data file
         - the default number of seconds for the time-to-live (TTL) file on resource records
    NS (Name Server) records
       - NS records are used by top-level domain servers to direct traffic to the content DNS
         server that contains the authoritative DNS records

     hellocloudgurus.com -> .com            -> NS Record            -> SOA
                             TLD                                    contains varies records
                        172800 IN NS ns.awsdns.com

  What is a Record
    A (address) Record
      - An A (address) record is the fundamental type of DNS record
      - used to translate the name of the domain to an IP address

  What is TTL (Time to Live)
     - the length that a DNS record is cached on either the resolving server or the
       user's own local PC is equal to the value of the Time To Live (TTL) in secords
     - the lower the time to live, the faster changes to the DNS records take to propagate
       throughout the internet
     - example, if planning to do a DNS migration, you need to lower TTL
       (e.g. lower TTL from 48 hours to 5 min a few days in advance)

  CNAME (canonical name)
    - can be used to resolve one domain name to another
    - for example, you man have a mobile website with the domain name http://m.acloud.guru that
      is used when users browse to your domain name on their mobile devices
      - you may also want the name http://mobile.acloud.guru to resolve to this same address

  Alias records
    - are used to map resource record sets to your hosted zone to load balancer, cloudFront distributions,
      or S3 buckets that are configured as websites
    - Alias records work like CNAME record in that you can map one DNS Name (www.example.com) to
      another "target" DNS Name (elb1234.elb.amazonaws.com)
    - Alias records are used only in the AWS eco system

  Alias records vs CNAME
    - CNAME cannot be used for naked domain names (zone apex record)
       - you can't have a CNAME for acloudguru.com. but can have for www.acloudguru.com
       - a naked domain name starts without "www."
       - CNAME cannot handle naked domains, AWS Route 53 uses Alias records to workaround this limitation

    - Alias Record can be used for a naked domain name / zone apex record

  Route 53
    - AWS DNS service
    - it allows you to register domain names, create hosted zones, and manage and create DNS records
    - Route 53 is name after Route 66 (original Highway across US), but is called 53 because DNS
      operates on port 53

  Route 53 7 Routing Policies
    1. Simple Routing
    2. Weighted Routing
    3. Latency-Based Routing
    4. Failover Routing
    5. Geolocation Routing
    6. Geoproximity Routing (traffic flow only)
    7. Multivalue Answer Routing

  Route 53 Exam Tips

      Alias Record vs CNAME
         - CNAME cannot map to a naked domain name (starts with "www.")
         - An alias record to map to naked domain name, Elastic Load balancer, S3 bucket
         - given the choice between Alias record and CNAME, choice Alias record
      Common DNS Record Types
        SOA (Start of Authority):
          - SOA record store information about
             - the name of the server that supplied the data for the zone
             - the administrator of the zone
             - the current version of the data file
             - the default number of seconds for the time-to-live (TTL) file on resource records
        NS (Name Server) records
           - NS records are used by top-level domain servers to direct traffic to the content DNS
             server that contains the authoritative DNS records
        CNAME records
          - where you can map one domain to another
        A (address) Records
           - maps a domain to IP address

9.2 Register a Domain Name

  Registering a Domain Name
    - you can buy a domain names directly with AWS
      - preferred method because it integrates with AWS Services and they
        will automatically setup a hosted zone for you
    - it can take up to 3 days to register depending on the circumstances
      (most cases within an hour)

   Notes:
      Route 53 is at a global level

   Console
     -> Route 53 -> enter domain name "phall.net" -> check -> add to cart -> continue
     -> enter contact info
     -> complete

    # provision a couple of ec2 instance
     N. Virginia
     -> EC2 -> AL2, t2.micro, user data: start apache, SG: WebDMZ

     Tyko
     -> EC2 -> AL2, t2.micro, user data: start apache with states in Tokyo, SG: WebDMZTokyo

---------------------------------------------------
9.3 Demo: Using a Simple Routing Policy

  Simple Routing Policy
      - you can have only one record with multiple IP addressess
      - if you specify multiple IP addresses in a record, Route 53 returns all values to the user in a ramdon order
      - do not work with Health Checks

                   Route 53 randomly picks the IP address to send traffic to:

                                  |------ 1st IP address ---> 30.0.0.1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 2nd IP address ---> 30.0.0.2

       - Use for a single resource that performs a certain role for your domain, for example, a web server that
         provides content to the example.com site

  Console Demo
    -> Predemo setup: need ec2 webservers in 2 regions, a Previously created / registered Domain Name with a Hosted Zone
    -> AWS console -> Networking ... -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com ->
       -> Currently Only 2 Records: NS and SOA
       -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Simple Routing, TTL: 60 (sec)
           Value:  <ec2 webserver Public IP us-east-1>
                   <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-northeast-1>
                   Note: Using Address record, and Not using a alias since pointing at two public IP addresses
                   -> Create Record
       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" many times, and see it is switching between EC2 webservers

  Exam Tips

    Simple Routing Policy
      - you can have only one record with multiple IP addressess
      - if you specify multiple IP addresses in a record, Route 53 returns all values to the user in a ramdon order
      - do not work with Health Checks

                   Route 53 randomly picks the IP address to send traffic to:

                                  |------ 1st IP address ---> 30.0.0.1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 2nd IP address ---> 30.0.0.2

       - Use for a single resource that performs a certain role for your domain, for example, a web server that
         provides content to the example.com site

---------------------------------------------------
9.4 Demo: Using a Weighted Routing Policy

  Weighted Routing Policy
      - in this example, Route 53 will send 20% of the traffic to us-east-1 and 80% of the traffic to us-west-1
      - it will sum the weightings then divide the individual weightings by the sum of the weightings
      - if you want to send a percentage of the traffic to specific Regions and/or AZs, think weighted Routing

                                  |------ 20%  ----> us-east-1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 80%  ----> us-west-1
  Route 53 Health Checks
    - you can set health checks on individual record sets
    - in a record set fails a health check, it will be removed from Route 53 until it passes the health check
    - You can set SNS notifications to alear you about failed Health Checks
    - You create health checks for the resources that you can't create alias records for, such as EC2 instances or
      servers in your own data center. You specify how to send health-checking requests to the resource: which protocol
      to use (HTTP, HTTPS, or TCP), which IP address and port to use, and, for HTTP/HTTPS health checks, a domain name and path.

  Console Demo
    -> Predemo setup: need ec2 webservers in 2 regions, a Previously created / registered Domain Name with a Hosted Zone
    -> AWS console -> Networking ... -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com ->
       -> Currently Only 3 Records: NS, SOA, A record with simple routing policy
          -> delete A record with "Simple" Routing Policy (from 9.3 demo)
       # Create a health check for each weighted Route - 1st N. Virginia EC2 webserver
       -> Route 53 Dashboard -> Health Checks -> Create Health Check ->
          Name: N-Va-HC, Monitor: Endpoint (e.g. IP address)
          Monitor Endpoint: Specify endpoint by: IP Address, IP Address: <ec2 webserver Public IP us-east-1>
          Host name: index.html    -> Next ->  Create alarm: No -> Create Health Check

       # Create a health check for each weighted Route - 2nd Tokyo EC2 webserver
       -> Route 53 Dashboard -> Health Checks -> Create Health Check ->
          Name: Tyko-HC, Monitor: Endpoint (e.g. IP address)
          Monitor Endpoint: Specify endpoint by: IP Address, IP Address: <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-northeast-1>
          Host name: index.html    -> Next ->  Create alarm: No -> Create Health Check


       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Weighted Routing, Weight: 30, Health check: N-VA-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: NV-Weight
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP us-east-1>
              Note: You can have multiple values (IP Addresses) for "Value"
              Note: Using Address record, and Not using a alias since pointing at a public IP addresses
                   -> Create Record

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Weighted Routing, Weight: 70, Health check: Tokyo-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: Tokyo-Weight
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-northeast-1>
              Note: You can have multiple values (IP Addresses) for "Value"
              Note: Using Address record, and Not using a alias since pointing at a public IP addresses
                   -> Create Record


       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" many times, and see it is switching between EC2 webservers
          where ~70% in Tokyo and ~30% in N. Virginia

  Exam Tips
    Weighted Routing Policy
      - in this example, Route 53 will send 20% of the traffic to us-east-1 and 80% of the traffic to us-west-1
      - it will sum the weightings then divide the individual weightings by the sum of the weightings
      - if you want to send a percentage of the traffic to specific Regions and/or AZs, think weighted Routing

                                  |------ 20%  ----> us-east-1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 80%  ----> us-west-1
    Route 53 Health Checks
    - you can set health checks on individual record sets
    - in a record set fails a health check, it will be removed from Route 53 until it passes the health check
    - You can set SNS notifications to alear you about failed Health Checks

9.5 Demo: Using a Failover Routing Policy

  Failover Routing Policy
       - use when you want to create an active/passive setup
          - for example, you may hwat your primary sit to be in eu-west-2 and your secondary [disaster recovery]
            site in ap-southwest-2
          - route 53 will monitor the health of your primary site using a health check

       - in the example, Route 53 will send the traffic to ap-southeast-2 because it has detected a failure in eu-west-2
         (failure detected by health check).

                                  |-XXX--Active----> eu-west-1 XXX
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------Passive---> ap-southeast-2

       - Use when you want to configure active-passive failover.

  Console Demo
    -> Predemo setup: need ec2 webservers in 2 regions, a Previously created / registered Domain Name with a Hosted Zone,
       and 2 health checks
    -> AWS console -> Networking ... -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com ->
       -> Currently Only 4 Records: NS, SOA, and 2 A record with weighted routing policy
          -> delete the two A record with "Weighted" Routing Policy (from 9.4 demo)

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Failover Routing,  Failover Record Type: Primary, Health check: N-VA-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: NV-FO
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP us-east-1>
                   -> Create Record

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Failover Routing, Failover Record Type: Secondary , Health check: Tokyo-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: Tokyo-FO
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-northeast-1>
                   -> Create Record

       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" and it show using N. Virginia

       -> to similate failure, go to EC2 N. Virginia Security Group, and remove HTTP (80) inbound traffic rule
          resulting the health check failure

       -> Route 53 -> Health Checks -> NV-HC failed (unhealthy)

       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" and it show using Tokyo

       -> fix security group for next demo, go to EC2 N. Virginia Security Group, and add HTTP (80) inbound traffic rules

       -> Now 4 Records: NS, SOA, and 2 A record with failover routing policy
          -> delete the two A record with "failover" Routing Policy (from this demo)

  Exam Tips
    Failover Routing Policy
       - use when you want to create an active/passive setup
          - for example, you may hwat your primary sit to be in eu-west-2 and your secondary [disaster recovery]
            site in ap-southwest-2
          - route 53 will monitor the health of your primary site using a health check

       - in the example, Route 53 will send the traffic to ap-southeast-2 because it has detected a failure in eu-west-2
         (failure detected by health check).

                                  |-XXX--Active----> eu-west-1 XXX
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------Passive---> ap-southeast-2

       - Use when you want to configure active-passive failover.

9.6 Demo: Using a Geolocation Routing Policy

  Geolocation Routing Policy
       - lets you choose where your traffic will be sent based on the  geographic location of your users
         (e.g. the location from which the DNS queries orginate)
       - in the example, Route 53 will send the European traffic to eu-west-1 and US User to us-east-1
       - example use cases: EU webserver configureed with Euros, XX Webservers Configured for specific languages


         European Users -> |                  |-- EU Users ----> eu-west-1
                           | --> Route 53 --->|
         US Users     ---->|                  |-- US Users ----> us-east-1

        - Use when you want to route traffic based on the location of user

  Geolocation Routing Policy Use Cases
    - if you want all queries from Europe to be routed to a fleet of EC2 instances that are configured fro your
      European Customers
    Localization
    - these serers may have the local language of your European customers and display all prices in euros

  Console Demo
    -> Predemo setup: need ec2 webservers in 2 regions, a Previously created / registered Domain Name with a Hosted Zone,
       and 2 health checks
    -> AWS console -> Networking ... -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com ->
       -> Currently Only 4 Records: NS, SOA, and 2 A record with weighted routing policy
          -> delete the two A record with "Weighted" Routing Policy (from 9.4 demo)

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Geolocation Routing,  Location: Europe, Health check: N-VA-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: Tokyo-Geo
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-northwest-1>
                   -> Create Record
           Note; Geolocation Routing Location can be based on Continent or Country

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Geolocation Routing,  Location: North America, Health check: N-VA-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: NV-Geo
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-northwest-1>
                   -> Create Record

       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" and it show using Tokyo (since demo is running in London)

  Exam Tips
    Geolocation Routing Policy
       - lets you choose where your traffic will be sent based on the  geographic location of your users
         (e.g. the location from which the DNS queries orginate)
       - in the example, Route 53 will send the European traffic to eu-west-1 and US User to us-east-1
       - example use cases: EU webserver configureed with Euros, XX Webservers Configured for specific languages


         European Users -> |                  |-- EU Users ----> eu-west-1
                           | --> Route 53 --->|
         US Users     ---->|                  |-- US Users ----> us-east-1

        - Use when you want to route traffic based on the location of user

---------------------------------------------------
9.7 Demo: Using a Geoproximity Routing Policy

  Route 53 Traffic Flow
    - use Route 53 traffic flow to build a routing system that uses a combination of:
       - geographic location
       - latency
       - and availability to route traffic
      from your users to your cloud or on-premises endpoints
    - you can build your traffic routing policies
      - from scratch
      - or you can pick a template from a library and then customizze it

  Geoproximity Routing Policy
      - to use geoproximity routing, you must use Route 53 traffic flow
      - Route 53 route traffic to your resources based on the geographic location of your users and your resources
      - you can optionally choose to route more traffic or less to a given resource by specifying a value, known as
        a bias.
      - A bias expands or shrinks the size of the geographic region from which traffic is routed to a resource

      - Use when you want to route traffic based on the location of your resources and optionally switch resource traffic
        at one location to resources elsewhere.

  Console Demo
    -> Predemo setup: need ec2 webservers in 2 regions, a Previously created / registered Domain Name with a Hosted Zone,
       and 2 health checks
    # 1st, create a traffic policy
    -> AWS console -> Networking ... -> Route 53 -> Traffic Policy -> Create Traffic Policy
       Policy Name: TrafPolicy1 -> Next -> Start point: DNS Type:  A Record, Connect to: weighted rule
          -> Fill out weighted rule -> Connect to ...


       Note: Connect To options include: Weighted Rule, Failover rule, Geolocation Rule, Latency Rule, Multivalue Anwser Rule,
                                         Geoproximity Rule, New endpoint, ...
             Import option used to: import JSON traffic rules

  Exam Tips

   Route 53 Traffic Flow
    - use Route 53 traffic flow to build a routing system that uses a combination of:
       - geographic location
       - latency
       - and availability to route traffic
      from your users to your cloud or on-premises endpoints
    - you can build your traffic routing policies
      - from scratch
      - or you can pick a template from a library and then customizze it

   Geoproximity Routing Policy
      - to use geoproximity routing, you must use Route 53 traffic flow
      - Route 53 route traffic to your resources based on the geographic location of your users and your resources
      - you can optionally choose to route more traffic or less to a given resource by specifying a value, known as
        a bias.
      - A bias expands or shrinks the size of the geographic region from which traffic is routed to a resource

      - Use when you want to route traffic based on the location of your resources and optionally switch resource traffic
        at one location to resources elsewhere.
---------------------------------------------------
9.8 Demo: Using a Latency Routing Policy

  Latency-based Routing Policy
      - allows you to route your traffic based on the lowest network latency for your end user (e.g. which region
        will give them the fastest response time)
      - in this example, South African User has 300ms latency to ap-southeast-2 and 54ms latency to eu-west-2,
        therefore Route 53 will send traffic to eu-east-2 because it has a much lower latency than ap-southeast-2
      - for exam questions about latency choice latency-based routing

                     Route 53 sends traffic to eu-west-2 becasue it has much lower latency

         South                      |------ 54ms ----> eu-west-2
         African -->  Route 53 ---> |
         User                       |------ 300ms ---> ap-southeast-2

      - Use when you have resources across multiple AWS regions and want to route traffic to the region
        that provides the best latency.

  Latency Resource Record Set
    - to use latency-based routing, you create a latency response record set for the ECS (or ELB) resource in
      each region that hosts your website
    - when Route 53 receives a query for you website, it selects the latency response record set for the
      the region that gives the user the lowest latency

  Console Demo
    -> Predemo setup: need ec2 webservers in 2 regions, a Previously created / registered Domain Name with a Hosted Zone,
       and 2 health checks
    -> AWS console -> Networking ... -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com ->
       -> Currently Only 2 Records: NS, and SOA

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Latency Routing,  Region: US East (N. Virginia) , Health check: N-VA-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: NV-Lat
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP us-east-1>
                   -> Create Record

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Latency Routing,  Region: Asia Pacific (Tokyo) , Health check: Tokyo-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: Tokyo-Lat
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-northwest-1>
                   -> Create Record


       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" and it show using N. Virginia since demo is running in London)


  Exam Tips

   Latency-based Routing Policy
      - allows you to route your traffic based on the lowest network latency for your end user (e.g. which region
        will give them the fastest response time)
      - in this example, South African User has 300ms latency to ap-southeast-2 and 54ms latency to eu-west-2,
        therefore Route 53 will send traffic to eu-east-2 because it has a much lower latency than ap-southeast-2
      - for exam questions about latency choice latency-based routing

                     Route 53 sends traffic to eu-west-2 becasue it has much lower latency

         South                      |------ 54ms ----> eu-west-2
         African -->  Route 53 ---> |
         User                       |------ 300ms ---> ap-southeast-2

      - Use when you have resources across multiple AWS regions and want to route traffic to the region
        that provides the best latency.

   Latency Resource Record Set
    - to use latency-based routing, you create a latency response record set for the ECS (or ELB) resource in
      each region that hosts your website
    - when Route 53 receives a query for you website, it selects the latency response record set for the
      the region that gives the user the lowest latency

---------------------------------------------------
9.9 Demo: Using a Multivalue [Answer] Routing Policy

  Multivalue [Answer] Routing Policy
       - essentially, simple routing with health checks
       - if 1st Record / IP Address fails health checks, traffic is redirected to next (2nd) Record / IP address

                                  |------ 1st Record / IP address ---> 30.0.0.1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 2nd Record / IP address ---> 30.0.0.2

        - Use when you want Route 53 to respond to DNS queries with up to eight randomly selected healthy records.

  Console Demo
    -> Predemo setup: need ec2 webservers in 2 regions, a Previously created / registered Domain Name with a Hosted Zone,
       and 2 health checks
    -> AWS console -> Networking ... -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com ->
       -> Currently Only 2 Records: NS, and SOA

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Multivalue Answer Routing,  Health check: N-VA-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: NV-MultiV
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP us-east-1>
                   -> Create Record

       -> Route 53 -> Hosted Zones -> select "hellocloudguru123.com" -> Create Record -> Quick Create Record ->
           Record Type: A record, Record Name: hellocloudguru123.com (naked domain name),
           Routing Policy: Multivalue Answer Routing,  Health check: Tokyo-HC, TTL: 60 (sec), Record ID: Tokyo-MultiV
           Value: <ec2 webserver Public IP ap-nortwest-1>
                   -> Create Record


       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" and it show using N. Virginia

       -> to similate failure, go to EC2 N. Virginia Security Group, and remove HTTP (80) inbound traffic rule
          resulting the health check failure

       -> Route 53 -> Health Checks -> NV-HC failed (unhealthy)

       -> Open browers and to to "hellocloudguru123.com" and it show using Tokyo

       -> fix security group for next demo, go to EC2 N. Virginia Security Group, and add HTTP (80) inbound traffic rules

  Exam Tips

    Multivalue [Answer] Routing Policy
       - essentially, simple routing with health checks
       - if 1st Record / IP Address fails health checks, traffic is redirected to next (2nd) Record / IP address

                                  |------ 1st Record / IP address ---> 30.0.0.1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 2nd Record / IP address ---> 30.0.0.2

        - Use when you want Route 53 to respond to DNS queries with up to eight randomly selected healthy records.

---------------------------------------------------
9.10 Route 53 Exam Tips

  Alias records vs CNAME
    - CNAME cannot be used for naked domain names (zone apex record)
       - you can't have a CNAME for acloudguru.com. but can have for www.acloudguru.com
       - a naked domain name starts without "www."
       - CNAME cannot handle naked domains, AWS Route 53 uses Alias records to workaround this limitation
       - Alias records can be used to translate a naked domain name (, subdomain names, etc) to an ELB, S3 Bucket, etc
       - CNAME only allows you to translate a subdomain name to another subdomain name (e.g. m.acloudguru.com to mobile.agloudguru.com)
    - Alias Record can be used for a naked domain name / zone apex record
    - Alias Records are unique to AWS/Route 53
    - for exam, given a choice between CNAME and Alias, choice Alias record

  Common DNS record Types
    SOA (Start of Authority):
      - SOA record store information about
         - the name of the server that supplied the data for the zone
         - the administrator of the zone
         - the current version of the data file
         - the default number of seconds for the time-to-live (TTL) file on resource records
    NS (Name Server) records
       - NS records are used by top-level domain servers to direct traffic to the content DNS
         server that contains the authoritative DNS records
    CNAME records
      - where you can map one domain to another
    A (address) Records
       - maps a domain to IP address

  7 Routing Policies with Route 53
    1. Simple Routing Policy
      - you can have only one record with multiple IP addressess
      - if you specify multiple IP addresses in a record, Route 53 returns all values to the user in a ramdon order
      - do not work with Health Checks

                   Route 53 randomly picks the IP address to send traffic to:

                                  |------ 1st IP address ---> 30.0.0.1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 2nd IP address ---> 30.0.0.2

       - Use for a single resource that performs a certain role for your domain, for example, a web server that
         provides content to the example.com site

    2. Weighted Routing Policy
      - in this example, Route 53 will send 20% of the traffic to us-east-1 and 80% of the traffic to us-west-1
      - it will sum the weightings then divide the individual weightings by the sum of the weightings
      - if you want to send a percentage of the traffic to specific Regions and/or AZs, think weighted Routing

                                  |------ 20%  ----> us-east-1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 80%  ----> us-west-1

    3. Latency-based Routing Policy
      - in this example, South African User has 300ms latency to ap-southeast-2 and 54ms latency to eu-west-2,
        therefore Route 53 will send traffic to eu-east-2 because it has a much lower latency than ap-southeast-2
      - for exam questions about latency choice latency-based routing

                     Route 53 sends traffic to eu-west-2 becasue it has much lower latency

         South                      |------ 54ms ----> eu-west-2
         African -->  Route 53 ---> |
         User                       |------ 300ms ---> ap-southeast-2

      - Use when you have resources across multiple AWS regions and want to route traffic to the region
        that provides the best latency.

    4. Failover Routing Policy
       - in the example, Route 53 will send the traffic to ap-southeast-2 because it has detected a failure in eu-west-2
         (failure detected by health check).

                                  |-XXX--Active----> eu-west-1 XXX
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------Passive---> ap-southeast-2
       - Use when you want to configure active-passive failover.

    5. Geolocation Routing Policy
       - in the example, Route 53 will send the European traffic to eu-west-1 and US User to us-east-1
       - example use cases: EU webserver configureed with Euros, XX Webservers Configured for specific languages


         European Users -> |                  |-- EU Users ----> eu-west-1
                           | --> Route 53 --->|
         US Users     ---->|                  |-- US Users ----> us-east-1

        - Use when you want to route traffic based on the location of user

    6. Geoproximity Routing Policy (Traffic Flow Only)
      - Route 53 route traffic to your resources based on the geographic location of your users and your resources
      - you can optionally choose to route more traffic or less to a given resource by specifying a value, known as
        a bias. A bias expands or shrinks the size of the geographic region from which traffic is routed to a resource
      - to use geoproximity routing, you must use Route 53 traffic flow

      - Use when you want to route traffic based on the location of your resources and optionally switch resource traffic
        at one location to resources elsewhere.

    7. Multivalue Answers Routing
       - if 1st Record / IP Address fails health checks, traffic is redirected to next (2nd) Record / IP address


                                  |------ 1st Record / IP address ---> 30.0.0.1
         Users -->  Route 53 ---> |
                                  |------ 2nd Record / IP address ---> 30.0.0.2

        - Use when you want Route 53 to respond to DNS queries with up to eight randomly selected healthy records.

  Registering A Domain Name with Route 53
    - you can buy domain names directly with AWS
    - it can take up to 3 days to register depending on the circumstances

  Route 53 Health Checks
    - you can set health checks on individual record sets
    - in a record set fails a health check, it will be removed from Route 53 until it passes the health check
    - You can set SNS notifications to alert you about failed Health Checks
    - You create health checks for the resources that you can't create alias records for, such as EC2 instances or
      servers in your own data center. You specify how to send health-checking requests to the resource: which protocol
      to use (HTTP, HTTPS, or TCP), which IP address and port to use, and, for HTTP/HTTPS health checks, a domain name and path.

  Route 53 using for S3 bucket for static web site
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/getting-started-s3.html
    1. Register a domain name, such as example.com
    2. Create an Amazon S3 bucket and configure it to host a website
     Note: The bucket name must always be the same as the domain name.
    3. Create a sample website and save the file in your S3 bucket
    4. Configure Amazon Route 53 to route traffic to your new website

9.11 AWS Certified Solutinon Architect - Associate (SAA-C03) Route 53

Question 1
True or False? Route 53 only provides DNS for public IPs.
Choices:
 - False
 - True
Answer: False
Route 53 allows you to host both public and private zones. In terms of IP addresses, public zones should be used for
public IPs and private zones should be used for private IPs. Reference Documentation:

Working with Private Hosted Zones
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/hosted-zones-private.html

  A private hosted zone is a container that holds information about how you want Amazon Route 53 to respond to DNS queries
  for a domain and its subdomains within one or more VPCs that you create with the Amazon VPC service
