13.7 Executing Batch Workloads Using AWS Batch

  AWS Batch on AWS Fargate
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/batch/latest/userguide/fargate.html#when-to-use-fargate
  AWS Batch FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/batch/faqs/
  What is AWS Fargate
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECS/latest/userguide/what-is-fargate.html

  AWS Fargate
    - serverless managed services that use can use with ECS to run containers without having
      to manage servers or clusters of EC2 instances

  AWS Batch Service Overview
    - plans, schedules, and executes your batch computing workloads using ECS (Elastic Container
      Service), EKS (Elastic Kubernete Service), and AWS Fargate with option to use spot instances
    - supports any job that can be executed as a Docker container
    - compute resources used are EC2 instances [for ECS and EKS] and AWS Fargate compute resources
    - For ECS, requires ECS Agent to be be installed on compute resources within your AWS
      Batch env. The ECS Agent is pre-installed in Managed compute resource
    - no additional charge for AWS batch. You pay for the ECS/EKS EC2 instances of AWS Fargate
    Batched Workloads
      - allows you to run batch computing workloads within AWS (run on EC2 or EC2/Fargate)
    Make Things Simplier
      - removes any heavy lifting for configuration and management or infrastructure requred
        for computing
    Automatically Provision and Scale
      - capable of provisioning accurately sized compute resources based on number of jobs
        submitted, and optimizes the distribution of workloads
    No Installation Required
      - allows you to skip installation and maintenance, so you can focus on obtaining
        and analyzing the results

  AWS Batch Important Components
    Jobs:
      - units of work that are submitted to AWS Batch (e.g. shell scripts, executables, and
        Docker images
    Job Definition:
      - specify how your jobs are to be run (e.g. resources required)
    Job Queues:
      - jobs get submitted to specific queues and reside there until scheduled to run in
        a compute environment
    Compute Environment:
      - set of managed or unmanagedd compute resources used to run your jobs (e.g. EC2, Fargate, ...)

  AWS Batch with Fargate or EC2 Compute Environments (ECS or EKS)
    - Fargate is the recommended way of launching most batch jobs using containers
       - Fargate launches and scales a little better and will more closely match your requirements
         for your containers needs. Less likely to over provision.

  Sometimes, EC2 is the best choice - below are scenarios requiring EC2 compute (ECS or EKS) instead of Fargate
     Custom AMI Required
       - Custom AMIs can only be run via EC2
     vCPU Requriment more than 16 vCPUs
        Note: training says more than 4 vCPUs, but referenced Fargate doc say more than 16 vCPUs
     Memory Requriment more than 120 GB
        Note: training says more than 30 GB, but referenced Fargate doc say more than 120 GB
     Using GPU or ARM Gravition CPU
       - if your jobs requires a GPU or ARM-based Graviton CPU, then they can only be run on EC2
         for AWS Batch
     linuxParaments
       - when using linuxParameters parameters, you must run on EC2 Compute
       - linuxParameters: the linux-specific options that are applied to the container, such as
         Linux KernelCapabilities
     Custom AMI Required
       - custom AMIs can only be run via EC2
     Number of Jobs
       - For a large number of jobs, its best to run on EC2. Dispatched at a higher rate (more
         concurrency) than Fargate

  AWS Batch or AWS Lambda
    Time Limits
      - Lambda has a 15 minute execution time limit
      - batch does not have a time limit
      - if your work load takes longer that 15 min, then you should use AWS Batch
    Disk Space
      - Lambda has limited disk space, and EFS requires functions live within a VPC
    Runtime (program version executables, e.g. python 3.8) Limits
      - Lambda is fully serverless, but it has natively limited runtimes
      - batch uses Docker, so any runtime can be used

  Managed and Unmanaged Compute Environments
    Managed Compute Environment
      - AWS manages capacity and instance types
      - Compute resources specs are defined when environment is created
      - ECS (Elastic Container Service) are launched into VPC subnets that you specify
        - ECS instance need access to the ECS service endpoints
        - By default, ECS will use the most recent and approved AWS ECS AMI
        - you can use your own AMI provided it meets the AWS ECS optimized image specification
        - leverage Fargate, Fargate Spot, and regular spot instances
    Unmanaged Compute Environment
      - you manage your own resources entirely
      - you must use an AMI that meets the AWS ECS optimized image specification
      - less commonly used compareed to managed compute env
      - Still, a good choice for extremely complex or specific requirements

  Exam Tips

    AWS Batch
      - plans, schedules, and executes your batch computing workloads using ECS (Elastic Container
        Service), EKS (Elastic Kubernete Service), and AWS Fargate with option to use spot instances
      Long-Running Workloads
        - perfect for long-running and event-driven workloads.
        - Anything requiring more that 15 min (AWS Lambda has 15 min execution limit)
      Managed Service
        - leverage AWS to handle the infrastructure creation and configurations

    AWS Batch Important Components
      Jobs:
        - units of work that are submitted to AWS Batch (e.g. shell scripts, executables, and
          Docker images)
      Job Definition:
        - specify how your jobs are to be run (e.g. resources required)
      Job Queues:
        - jobs get submitted to specific queues and reside there until scheduled to run in
          a compute environment
      Compute Environment:
        - set of managed or unmanaged compute resources used to run your jobs (e.g. EC2, Fargate, ...)

    AWS Batch or AWS Lambda
      - Lambda has limitations on execution time limits, file storage size, and runtimes
      Execution Time Limits
        - Lambda has a 15 minute execution time limit
        - batch does not have a time limit
        - if your work load takes longer that 15 min, then you should use AWS Batch
      File Storage / Disk Size
        - Lambda has limited disk size, and EFS requires functions live within a VPC
      Runtime (program version executables, e.g. python 3.8) Limits
        - Lambda is fully serverless, but it has natively limited runtimes
        - batch uses Docker, so any runtime can be used

    What type of compute?
      - use cases determine when to use Fargate or ECS EC2 instances
      - consider the number of jobs and specific resource requirements (e.g. memory and GPU)

    Managed vs Unmanaged
      Managed
        - leverage AWS for managing capacity and compute
        - they are the most common
      UnManaged
        - you manage everything
        - they are used for specific and complex scenarios

---------------------------------------------------
13.8 Brokering Messages with Amazon MQ

  Amazon MQ (messaging queue) FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/amazon-mq/faqs/

  Amazon MQ (messaging queue) Features
    https://aws.amazon.com/amazon-mq/features/

   Amazon MQ Industry-standard APIs and Protocols
     https://aws.amazon.com/amazon-mq/features/#Industry-standard_APIs_and_Protocols

  Amazon MQ Overview
    Message Broker
     - message broker service allowing easier migration of existing applications to AWS
    Support a Variety prog languates, OS, & APIs
      - leverages multiple programming languages, operating systems, and messaging protocols
    Engine Types
      - Supports Apache ActiveMQ and RabbitMQ engines
    Managed Service
      - allows you to easily leverage exist apps without managing and maintaining your own system

  SNS with SQS vs Amazon MQ
    Topics and Queues
      - Each offers architectures with topics and queues.
      - Allows for one-to-one or one-to-many message designs
    Exiting Messaging Applications
      - if migrating existing applications with messaging systems in place, you likely want to
        consider MQ
    New Messaging Applications
      - if creating new applications, look at SNS and SQS: simplier to use, highly scalable, and
        and simple APIs.
    Public Accesibility
      - AWS MQ requires private network like VPC, Direct Connect, or VPN
      - SNS and SQS are publicly accessible by default
    AWS default Integrations
      - Amazon MQ has NO default AWS integrations, but:
      - according to "Amazon MQ FAQs", "any application that runs on an AWS compute service (EC2, ECS,
        or Lambda) has MQ integration. MQ is integrated to CloudWatch, CloudWatch Logs, CloudTrail,
        CloudFormation, IAM, and KMS.

  Configuring Brokers
    Single-Instance Broker
      - one broker lives within one AZ
      - perfect use for development environments
      - RabbitMQ has a network Load Balancer in front
    Highly Available
      - MQ offers highly available arhitectures to minimize downtime during maintenance.
      - Architecture depends on the broker engine type
    Amazon MQ for ActiveMQ
      - with active/standby deployments, one instance will remain available at all times
      - Configure network of brokers with separate maintenance windows
    Amazon MQ for RabbitMQ
      - cluster deployments are logical grouping of 3 broker nodes across multiple AZs sitting
        behind a Network Load balancer

  Exam Tips

  Amazon MQ
    Message Broker Service
     - message broker service allowing easier migration of existing applications to AWS
    Engine Types
      - Supports Apache ActiveMQ and RabbitMQ engines
    Specific Messaging Protocols
      - anything specific to JMS or messaging protocols like AMQP 0-9-1. AMQP 1.0, MQTT,
        OpenWire, and STOMP
          Notes:
            - JMS (Java Message Service): nessagug standard that allows appliction components based on
              Java Platform Enterprise Edition (Java EE) to create, send, receive, and read messages
            - AMQP (Advanced Message Queuing Protocol) is core protocol for RabbitMQ, but it also
              supports STORM, MQTT, HTTP through use of plugins. HTTP is not messaging protocol, but
              RabbitMQ plugins can ues HTTP to send/receive messages
            - OpenwWire allow native access ActiveMQ from a number of different languages and platforms
            - MQTT (Message Queuing Telemetry Transpport) a publish/subscribe machine-to-machine network
            - STOMP (Streaming/Simple Text Orientated Message Protocol) simple (or streaming) text orientated
              messaging protocol protocol for messaging queue. Used with IoT, etc.

    Managed Service
      - allows you to easily leverage exist apps without managing and maintaining your own system
    Support a Variety prog languates, OS, & APIs
      - leverages multiple programming languages, operating systems, and messaging protocols


  SNS with SQS vs Amazon MQ
    Exiting Messaging Applications
      - if migrating existing applications with messaging systems in place, you likely want to
        consider MQ
    New Messaging Applications
      - if creating new applications, look at SNS and SQS: simplier to use, highly scalable, and
        and simple APIs.
    Public Accesibility
      - AWS MQ requires private network like VPC, Direct Connect, or VPN
      - SNS and SQS are publicly accessible by default

  Single-Instance Brokers or Highly Available
    Single-Instance Broker
      - one broker lives within one AZ
      - perfect use for development environments
      - RabbitMQ has a network Load Balancer in front
    Highly Available
      - each engine type (ActiveMQ and RabbitMQ) offer their own type highly available configurations
      - MQ offers highly available arhitectures to minimize downtime during maintenance.

13.9 Coordinating Distributed Apps with AWS Step Functions

  AWS Step Functions Overview
    Orchestration
      - Serverless orchestration service combining Lambda functions with different AWS
        services for business applications
    Graphical Console
      - comes with a graphical console for easier application workflow views and flow
    Main components within Step functions
      - main components are state machines and tasks
    State Machine (application flow)
      - a particular workflow with different event-driven steps
    Task
      - specific states within a workflow (state machine) representing a single unit of work
    States
      - every single step within a workflow is considered a state

  Executions
    Workflows - 2 types:
      - each workflow has executions
      - Executions are instances where you run your workflows in to perform your tasks
      Standard
        - have an exactly-once execution
        - can run for up to one year
        - useful for long-running workflows that need to have auditable history
        - rates up to 2000 executions per second
        - pricing based per state transition
      Express
        - At-least-once workflow execution
        - can run up to five minutes
        - useful for high-event-rate workloads
        - Example use case is IoT data streaming and ingestion
          Note: Data ingestion is the process of obtaining and importing data for immediate use or
                storage in a data base
        - Pricing based on the number of executions, durations, and memory consumed

  States and State Machines
    Flexible
      - leverage states to either make decisions based on input, perform certain actions, or pass output
    Language
      - states and workflows are defined in Amazon States Language (ASL)
    States
      - states are elements within your state machines
      - they are referred to by a name (any unique string within the scope of the entire state machine)
    Example use case
      - online pickup order - each step in that flow is considered a state

  Integrated AWS Services that work well with AWS Step Function
     Lambda, Batch, ECS/AWS Fargate, SNS, SQS, API Gateway, EventBridge, DynamoDB, etc.

  Different States
    Pass State
      - passes any input directly to its output - no work done
    Task State
      - single unit of work performed (e.g. Lambda, Batch, SNS)
    Choice State
      - adds branching logic to state machines
    Wait State
      - creates a specified time delay within the state machine
    Succeed State
      - stops executions successfully
    Fail State
      - stops executions and marks them as failures
    Parallel State
      - runs parallel branches of executions within state machines
    Map State
      - runs a set of steps based on elements of an input array

  Exam Tips


    AWS Step Functions
      What is AWS Step Functions:
        - Serverless orchestration service meant for event-driven task executions combining Lambda
          functions with different AWS services for business applications
        - comes with a graphical interface
      Execution Types:
        Standard: good for long-running, auditable executions
        Express workflows: good for high-event-rate executions
      Amazon States Language (ASL)
        - all state machines are written in the Amazon States Language format
        - proprietary format similar to JSON
      States
        - elements within your state machines
        - these are things and actions that happen with workflows
      Integrated AWS Services that work well with AWS Step Function
         Lambda, Batch, ECS/AWS Fargate, SNS, SQS, API Gateway, EventBridge, DynamoDB, etc.
      State Types:
        - there are currently 8 different state types: Pass, Task, Choice, Wait, Succeed, Fail, Parallel, and Map

---------------------------------------------------
13.10 Ingesting Data from SaaS (Software as a Service) Applications to AWS with Amazon AppFlow

   What is AppFlow
     Integration
       - fully managed integration service for exchanging data between SaaS apps and AWS services
         e.g.
            migrating data from Sale Force to S3;
            ingesting Sales contact record to Redshift table
            pulling Zendest support tickets and storing in an S3 Bucket
     Ingest Data
       - pulls data records from 3rd part SaaS vendors and stores them in S3
     Bi-Directional
       - bi-directional data transfers with limited combinations

   Important Terms and Concepts
     Flow
       - flows transfer data between sources and destinations
       - a variety of SaaS applications are supported
     Data Mapping
       - Determines how your source data is stored within your destinations
     Filters
       - Criteria to control which data is transferred from a source to a destination
     Trigger
       - how the flow is started
       - support trigger types: run on demand, run on event, run on schedule

   Example Diagram
          source           Source/Destination Connection       Destination
      |----------------|  <--------------------------    |---------------------|
      | 3rd Party      |   Map Fields                    |   AWS Services      |
      |  SaaS          |  <--------------------------    | e.g. S3 Buckets,    |
      |                |   Create Filters                | Redshift Database   |
      |                |  <--------------------------    |                     |
      |                |   Run Flow               Mapped Ingested Data --> S3  |
      |----------------|  -------------------------->    |---------------------|


   AppFlow Use Cases
     1. Transferring SalesForce records to AWS Redshift
     2. Ingesting and analyzing Slack conversations in S3
        Note: Slack is a messaging app for business

     3. Migrating Zendesk and other help desk support tickets to Snowflake
        Note: AWs Snowflake is cloud data warehouse
              Zendesk is a cloud-based customer service platform
     4. Transferring aggregate data on a scheduled basis to S3 (up to 100GB per flow)

   Exam Tips

   What is AppFlow
     Integration
       - fully managed integration service for transferring data to and from SaaS vendors and AWS services
         e.g.
            migrating data from Sale Force to S3;
            ingesting Sales contact record to Redshift table
            pulling Zendest support tickets and storing in an S3 Bucket
     Bi-Directional
       - Flows can be bi-directional between AWS services and SaaS applications

     Must ensure your application is supported!

   Exam Scenarios
     - Watch for solutions neediing easy (managed) and fast transfer of SaaS or 3rd party
       vendor data into AWS, especially if you need a schedule basis
     - Example: An application needs to reference large amounts of SaaS data regularly, and the
       data needs to be accessed from with S3

13.11 Decoupling Workflows Exam Tips

   Asynchronous messaging and event passing
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/whitepapers/latest/microservices-on-aws/asynchronous-messaging-and-event-passing.html


   Amazon SQS exam tips
   4 Questions to Ask in the Exam:
     1. Are the workloads Synchronous or asynchronous?
     2. What type of decoupling makes sense?
       - do you need a like pub/sub where you have a SNS topic pushing messages to several different queues?
       - do you need a step function which has ordering of workflows
     3. does the order of messages matter?
       - do you need a FIFO SQS queue that guarantees the ordering?
       - can you handle some duplications and ordering changes?
     4. What type of application load will you see?
       - many of these services have limits which can impact service you should use


   What to know for the Exam:
     SQS Exam Tips:
       SQS can duplicate messages:
         - this is only once in a while, so if it's happening consistently, check for misconfigured visibility
           timeout or if the developer is failing to make the delete API call
       Queues are not bidirectional:
         - if you need communication to return to the instance that sent the message, you need a 2nd queue
       Know the defaults
          - it's important to understand the standard values for all of SQS settings
            e.g. visibility timeout
            SQS Visibility Timeout
              - default Visibility timeout is 30 sec
              - after backend resource receives the message, the SQS queue locks out the message for the
                Visibility Timeout period (no other resource has access to the message during the lock out)
              - if backend does NOT respond in the Visibility Timeout period to purge the message, the message
                reappears in the SQS queue and it is available for other backend resources
       Nothing lasts forever:
          - message store in SQS can persist up to 14 days
            SQS Message Retention
              - default is 4 days
              - can be set between 4 minutes and 14 days
              - after message retention period, message exceeding this period are purged
       Does order matter
         - if message ordering is important, make sure to select SQS FIFO

     SNS and API Gateway
       Proactive Notifications = SNS
         - any time a question asks about email, text, or any type of push-based notification, think of SNS
       CloudWatch with SNS
         - scenarios that talk about getting a notification from cloudWatch alarm should immediately make
           you look for SNS in the (when versioning is enabled) answer
       API Gateway
         - you don't need to have an in-depth understanding.
         - you only need to know it acts as a secure front door to external communications coming in your
           environment

     AWS Simple Workflow Service
        - used to help you organize and process a sequential order of events. It can be used to track and
          manage your workflow of your processes.
        - helps developers build, run, and scale background jobs that have parallel or sequential steps.
          You can think of Amazon SWF as a fully-managed state tracker and task coordinator in the Cloud.
        - SWF offers several features that facilitate application development, such as passing data between tasks,
          signaling, and flexibility in distributing tasks
        - SWF task and workflow execution can last up to one year, and can include tasks to be performed by
          on-premises servers and humans.
        - SWF manages workflow state in the cloud. However, unlike Step Functions, a user has to manage the
          infrastructure that runs the workflow logic and tasks.
        - Tasks can run inside EC2 instances or on any server, including on premises and on other clouds

     AWS Batch
       Long-running, batch workloads
         - anything related to batch workloads that is long running (> 15 min) will likely involve AWS Batch
         - AWS Lambda has an execution time limit of 15 min
       Queued workloads
         - if you see a question about batch workloads requiring queues, then think AWS Batch
       On-demand alternative to AWS Lambda
         - questions regarding an alternative solution to AWS lambda due to runtime requirements or execution
           time limits, it could likely involve AWS Batch

     AWS Batch or AWS Lambda
       - Lambda has limitations on execution time limits, file storage size, and runtimes
       Execution Time Limits
         - Lambda has a 15 minute execution time limit
         - batch does not have a time limit
         - if your work load takes longer that 15 min, then you should use AWS Batch
       File Storage / Disk Size
         - Lambda has limited disk size, and EFS requires functions live within a VPC
       Runtime (program version executables, e.g. python 3.8) Limits
         - Lambda is fully serverless, but it has natively limited runtimes
         - batch uses Docker, so any runtime can be used

     Amazon MQ (message queue)
       Managed messaging broker
         - if there is any mention of a managed messaging broker service, think of Amazon MQ
       Engine Types: RabbitMQ and ActiveMQ
         - any mention of these two technologies means you should look for MQ
       Specific messaging protocols:
        - anything specific to JMS or messaging protocols like AMQP 0-9-1. AMQP 1.0, MQTT,
          OpenWire, and STOMP, then MQ has to be the anwser
        - SNS and SQS cannot handle these specific protocols
            Notes:
              - JMS (Java Message Service): nessagug standard that allows appliction components based on
                Java Platform Enterprise Edition (Java EE) to create, send, receive, and read messages
              - AMQP (Advanced Message Queuing Protocol) is core protocol for RabbitMQ, but it also
                supports STORM, MQTT, HTTP through use of plugins. HTTP is not messaging protocol, but
                RabbitMQ plugins can ues HTTP to send/receive messages
              - OpenwWire allow native access ActiveMQ from a number of different languages and platforms
              - MQTT (Message Queuing Telemetry Transpport) a publish/subscribe machine-to-machine network
              - STOMP (Streaming/Simple Text Orientated Message Protocol) simple (or streaming) text orientated
                messaging protocol protocol for messaging queue. Used with IoT, etc.

     AWS Step Functions
       Serverless Orchestration Service
         - any question with this saying will likely involve Step Functions
       Different workflow decision requirements
         - whenever the solution requires different states or logic during workflows, (e.g condition checks,
           failure catches, or especially wait periods), think of Step Functions
         - if question talks about a wait period up to 1 year, think of Step Functions

     Amazon AppFlow
       SaaS data ingestion:
         - For architectures requiring simplified data ingestion from external SaaS application in to AWS services,
           think of AppFlow
       Bi-directional
         - this services can be bi-directionall in certain use cases
         - data can be ingested into or out of an AWS service depending on the configurations
       Common uses cases:
          - 3rd party SaaS application needs to ingest data to S3, Redshift, AWS snowflake, etc.

Chapter 14 Big Data

14.1 Exploring Large Redshift Data

   Redshift FAQs:
     https://aws.amazon.com/redshift/faqs/

  The 3 V's of Big Data
    https://aws.amazon.com/big-data/what-is-big-data
    volumes
      - ranges from terabytes to petabytes of data
    Variety
      - includes data from a wide range of sources and formats
    Velocity
      - businesses required speed
      - data needs to be collected, stored, processed, and analyized within a short
        period of time
  What is Redshift?
    - a fully managed, petabyte-scale data warehouse service in the cloud
    - it's a very large relational database traditionally used in big data applications

  Redshift Uses
    Size
      - it can hold up to 16 PB of data
      - this means you do not have to split up your large datasets
    Relational
      - redshift is a relational database
      - you use your standard SQL and business intelligence (BI) tools to interact with it
    Usage
      - while redshift is a fantastic tool for BI (Business Intelligence) applications, it is
        not a replacment for standard RDS databases
      - not a great tool for the backend of your web application
  Console Demo
     -> console -> analytics -> Redshift -> create cluster
       cluster identifier: myRedshift, type: production, nodes types: dc2.large, Number of Nodes: 1, admin user name, password: ???
       create

       Note:
         - Redshift has Single-AZ and Multi-AZ deployments but Multi-AZ is only in preview mode 11/30/22
           https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2022/11/amazon-redshift-multi-az-preview-ra3-clusters/
            Single-AZ deployment is not highly available
            Single-AZ deployment:
              All Amazon Redshift clusters are created and situated in a particular Single-AZ
            Multi-AZ deployment:
              - provisioned in multiple AZs simultaneously.
              - automatically selects two subnets from two different Availability Zones and deploys
                an equal number of compute nodes in each AZ
              - All these compute nodes are utilized via a single endpoint as compute nodes from
                both AZs are used for workload processing.
         - can query database from provided endpoints or the console

  Exam Tips
      Redshift
       - Know the Redshift basics
       - big - store up to 16 PB of data (much larger than you standard RDS database storage)
       - its relational database for business intelligence (BI) applications
       - not standard: do not use Redshift in place of RDS

14.2 Processing Data with EMR (Elastic MapReduce)

   What is ETL (Extract, transform, load)

   What is EMR (Elastic MapReduce)
     - is a managed big data platform that allows you to process vast amounts of data using open-source
       tools, such as Spark, Hive, HBase, Flink, Hudi, and Presto
     - it is a open-source managed ETL tool
     - it's AWS's ETL tool
   EMR Architecture
     - can run on EC2, EKS, Outpost, etc

     EMR Service -->|      |-------------------------
                    | Internet  VPC                 |
                    |  Gateway                      |
     S3 Bucket <----|      |         ec2 cluster    |
                           --------------------------
   Console Demo
     -> Analytics -> EMR - Create Cluster
        Name: myEMR, EMR Version, Application Bundle: Spark, OS: Amazon Linux 2, Primary EC2 type
        set vpc, subnet, security group
        create
        -> 10+ min

        -> select -> Application User Interface -> select -> load in your ETL jobs
        -> Examine EC2 -> you will see EC2 instances started by EMR

   Exam Tips
      EMR (Elastic MapReduce)
        - it's an open-source cluster
          - EMR is a managed fleet of EC2 instances running open-source tools for
            ETL (Extract/Transform/load) workloads
          - similar to other open-source managed architecture in that it gets the
            fleet up and running quickly, but the technology isn't proprietary to AWS
         EC2 rules apply
           - you can use Reserved Instances (RI) and spot instance to reduce you costs
         EMR High Level
           - EMR is used to transform (processs) and move data
           - To improve the EMR peformance, adjust the number of simultaneous mapper tasks
         VPC
           - the EMR architecture lives inside a VPC
         Open-source Ecosystem
            EMR supports Apache Hadoop ecosystem components like Spark, Hive, HBase and Presto, with
            data storage in Amazon Athena, Amazon Redshift, and other big data analytics solutions

14.3 Streaming data with Kinesis

   What is Kinesis
     - allows you to ingest, process, and analyze real-time streaming data
     - a huge data highway connected to your AWS account
     - 2 Versions: Data Streams and Data Firehose

   Kinesis Data Streams
     Purpose
       - real-time streaming for ingesting data
       - unlimited possibility of where you can send the data to
     Speed
       - real-time
     Difficulty
       - you are responsible for creating the consumer and scaling the stream

   Kinesis Data Firehose
     Purpose
       - data transfer tool to get information to S3, Redshift, Elasticsearch, or Splunk
       - limited endpoints that you can send the data to
     Speed
       - near real-time (within 60 sec)
     Difficulty
       - plug and play with AWS architecture

   Kinesis Data Streams Architecture

       data producers:                                          data Consumers
         EC2 instances         -|---- Kinesis Data streams |       EC2 instance   ---> S3
         client                -|       shard1             |       EC2 instance   ---> DynamoDB
         mobile client         -|       shard2             |       EC2 instance   ---> Redshift
         tranditional server   -|        . . .             |       EC2 instance   ---> EMR
                                        shardN             |       EC2 instance   ---> Data Firehose

        Comsumer: process the data to put in the endpoint
                  with Kinesis Data Streams, you need to create/write the consumers
        shards: processing unit, and you need to decide on how many shards
                with Kinesis Data Streams, you need to handle the shards scaling


   Kinesis Data Firehost Architecture
     - handles the scaling for you
     - handles building out the consumer for you
     - limited 3rd party endpoint support (e.g. Splunk)

                                 | Elasticsearch
       input -> Data Firehose -> | S3
                                 | Redshift

   Kinesis Data Analytics and SQL
      Kinesis Data Analytics
        - can be paired with Kinesis Data Streams or Kinesis Firehose to analyze the data
        - analyze data using standard SQL
        Easy
          - simple to tie Data Analytics into your Kinesis pipeline
          - it's directly supported by Data Firehose and Data Streams
        No Servers
          - fully managed, real-time serverless service
          - it will automatically handle scaling and provisioning of needed resources
        Cost
          - you only pay for the amount of resources you consume as you data passes through
        Easy
   Kinesis vs SQS
      When we are looking for a message brokewr, which do we pick?
      SQS
        - a messaging broker that is simple to use and doesn't require much configuration
        - it doesn't offer real-time message delivery
      Kinesis
        - more complicated to configure and is mostly used in big data applications
        - it does provide real-time communication

   Exam Tips

     Real Time? Think Kinesis
       - there are very few services in AWS that offer "real-time" processing
       - Kinesis is one of the fiew services that offers this speed
       Near real-time
         - think kinesis Firehose
       real-time
         - think kinesis Data streams

      Kinesis
        Kinesis vs SQS
          - both are message brokers, but only Kinesis is real time
        Transforming Data
          - "Kinesis Data Analytics" is the easiest way to process data going through Kinesis using SQL
        Kinesis Scaling
          - Data Streams does NOT automatically scale
          - Data Firehose DOES automatically scale
        Kinesis vs SQS

14.4 Amazon Athena an AWS Glue

  What is Athena
    - an interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data in S3 using SQL
    - This allows you to directly query data in your S3 bucket without loading it into a database
    - don't have to load data into database to search

  What is AWS Glue
    - serverless ETL service
    - a serverless data integration service that makes it easy to discover, prepare, and combine data
    - It allowss you to perform ETL (Extract/Transform/Load) wokloads without managing underlying servers
    - kinda replacing EMR (Elastic MapReplace)

  Using Athena and AWS Glue together diagram


     S3 ----->   AWS Glue   ------> AWS Glue Data ------> Athena  ------> QuickSight
                 Crawlers             Catalog
                                        |
                                        -------------> Redshift Spectrum

     Redshift Spectrum: Allows you to use Redshift without loading all the data into a Redshift database

     Amazon QuickSight:
        - AWS version of Tableau
        - business analytics service to visualizations, perform ad-hoc analysis, and quickly get
          business in-sights from data, anytime on any devices
        - upload CSV and excel files, connect to SaaS applications like Salesforce; access on-premises
          databases like SQL server, MySQL, and PostgreSQL


  Exam Tips

     Athena:
        Serverless SQL option to query S3 data:
         - Athena is your best choice for serverless SQL solution
         - Athena is the only service that allows you to directly query your data that stored in S3
         - Athena queries could be for BI applications or logging data

     Athena and AWS Glue:
       Serverless:
         Both solutions are fully managed serverless services
       Overview:
         - Athena is serverless SQL
         - Glue is serverless ETL
       Better Together:
         - while Athena can work by itself, Glue can design a schema for your data


     Notes: Glue is NOT a database! Athena is NOT for querying RDS!

     - a fully managed business Intelligence (BI) data visualization service

          - think Quicksight

14.6  Moving Transformed Data Using AWS Data Pipeline

   AWS Data Pipeline Definition
     - a managed Extract, Transform, Load (ETL) service for automating movement and transformation
       of your data

   AWS Data Pipeline Overview
     Data Driven
       - define data-driven workflows
       - steps are dependent on previous tasks completing successfully
     Parameters
       - define your parameters for data transformation
       - AWS Data pipeline enforces you chosen logic
     Highly Available
       - AWS hosts the infrastructure on highly available and distributed infrastructure.
       - also fault tolerant
     Handling Failures
       - automatically retries failed activities
       - configure notifications via SNS for failures or even successful tasks
     AWS Storage services
       - integrates easily with DynamoDB, RDS, Redshift, and S3, ...
     AWS Compute
       - works with EC2 and EMR for compute needs

   Data Pipeline Components to Know
     Pipeline Definition
       - specify the business logic of your data management needs
       - what you need done and how do you need it to be completed
     Managed Compute
       - Service will create EC2 instances to perform your activities - or leverage existing EC2
     Tasks Runners
       - Task Runner (EC2 instances) poll for different tasks and perform them when they find
         them in the pipeline
     Data Nodes
       - define the locations and types of data that will be input and output
     Activities
       - pipeline components that define the work to perform

   Popular Use Cases
     1. Processing data in EMR using Hadoop streaming
     2. importing or exporting DynamoDB data
     3. Copying CSV files or data between S3 Buckets
     4. Exporting RDS data to S3
       - can set a trigger to export your RDS table data to S3 and notify you when it is completed
     5. Copying data to Redshift [so you can analyze it further]

   Example Diagram
      use case: Exporting MySQL data to S3 to Generate Reports


          --------------  Data Pipeline -------------------

   resouces:

             EC2  ----------->   RDS ------------->   S3  ------------>  EMR
                  Authenticate       export data           Generate
                  into Database      using task            Reports using
                  so task runner     runners               EMR
                  can log into
                  database, ...

         can schedule task: e.g. daily authenticate, daily export data, weekly generate reports

   Exam Tips

     AWS Data Pipeline
       Managed ETL
       - Managed AWS service for ETL workflows that automates movement and transformation of your data
       Data driven service
         - use data-driven workflows to create dependencies between tasks and activities
       Storage integrations
         - there are several integrations, including DynamoDB, RDS, Redshift, and S3, ...
       Compute integrations
       - easily integrates with EC2 and EMR for managed compute needs
       SNS
         - leverage SNS for any failure notifications
         - use it for successes and other event-driven workload notifications as well
       Keywords
         - anything related to managed ETL services, automatic retries for data-driven workflows
       Managed ETL

14.7 Implementing Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (Amazon MSK)

  Amazon Managed Streaming Apache Kafka (MSK) overview
    Apache Kafka
      - a fully managed service for running data streaming applications that leverage Apache Kafka
    Kafka Control Plane operations
      - provides control-plan operations
      - Creates, updates, and deletes clusters as required
    Kafka Data Plane operations
      - leverage Kafka data-plane operations for producing and consuming streaming data
    Existing Applicaitons
      - open-source versions of Apache Kafka allow support for existing apps, tools, and plugins

  MSK Important Components and Concepts
    Broker Nodes
      - specify the amount of broker nodes per AZ you at time of cluster creation
    ZooKeeper Nodes
      - ZooKeeper nodes are created for you
    Producers, Consumers, and Topics
      - Kafka data-plane operations allow creation of topics and ability to produce/consume data
    Flexible Custer Operations
      - perform cluster operations with the AWS console, AWS CLI, or APIs within any SDK
      - since it handles the control-plane, it will handle custer operations for you, or you
        can you can help it using AWS console, CLI< or APIs

  MSK Resiliency
    Automatic Recovery
      - automatic detection and recovery form common failure scenarios. Minimal Impact
    Detect Boker failures
      - detect broker failures results in mitigation resplacemnet of unhealthy nodes
    Reduce Data
      - tries to reuse storage from older brokers during failures to reduce data needing
        replication
    Time Required
      - impact time is limited to however long it takes MSK to complete detection and recovery
    After Recovery
      - after successful recovery, producers and consumer apps continue to communicate with the
        same broker IP address as before

  MSK Features
    MSK Serverless
      https://www.serverlessguru.com/tips/amazon-msk-vs-amazon-msk-serverless
      - is a cluster type for Amazon MSK that makes it easy for you to run Apache Kafka without having
        to manage and scale cluster capacity.
      - MSK Serverless automatically provisions and scales compute and storage resources, so you can use
        Apache Kafka on demand and pay for the data you stream and retain.
      - Custer types within Amazon MSK offering serverless clusters management
      - Automatic provisioning and scaling
    MSK Serverless is fully compatible with Apache Kafka
      - MSK serverless is fully compatible with Apache Kafka
      - use the same client apps for producing and consuming data
    MSK Connect
      https://aws.amazon.com/msk/features/msk-connect/
      - allows developers to easily stream data to and from Apache Kafka clusters
      - allows developers to move data between MSK clusters and external systems such as databases, file systems,
        and search indices.
      Note:
        Kafka Connect, an open source component of Apache Kafka, is a framework for connecting Apache Kafka
        with external systems such as databases, key-value stores, search indexes, and file systems.

  MSK Security and Logging
    1. Integration with KMS for SSE (server side encryption) requirements
    2. MSK will encrypt your data at rest by default
    3. By default, TLS 1.2 for encryption in transit between brokers in clusters
    4. Broker logs can be delivered to CloudWatch, S3, and Kinesis Data Firehose
    5. By default, MSK will connect metrics and sent them to CloudWatch
    6. All MSK API calls are logged to AWS CloudTrail

  Exam Tips

     Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (MSK)
       Apache Kafka
       - fully managed AWS service for running and building Apache Kafka data streaming applications
       Kafka Control Plane operations
         - service handles control-plan operations: Creation, updating, and deletion clusters
       Kafka Data Plane operations
         - leverage Kafka data-plane operations for producing and consuming streaming data
       Automatic Recoveries
         - service detects and automatically mitigates most of the common failures
       Encryption
         - integrates with KMS for SSE needs
         - By default, uses TLS 1.2 fo in-transit communications
       Logging
         - allows for pushing broker logs to CloudWatch, S3, or Kinesis Data Firehose
         - API calls are logged to CloudTrail

14.8 Analyzing Data with Amazon OpenSearch Service


   Introducing OpenSearch (Formerly ElasticSearch)
      https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/opensource/introducing-opensearch/

  Opensearch Overview
    - a managed service allowing you to run search and analytics engine for various use cases
    - it is the successfor to Amazon ElasticSearch service

  Opensearch Features
    Quick Analyzsis
      - quickly ingest, search, and analyze data in your custers
      - Commonly part of an ETL process
    Scalable
      - easily scalabe infrastructure running the open-source openSearch service
    Security
      - leverage IAM for access control, VPC security groups, encryption at rest and
        in-transit, and field-level security
    Stability
      - Multi-AZ capable service with several different master nodes and automated snapshots
    Flexible
      - allows for SQL support for Business Intelligence (BI) apps
    Integrations
      - Easily integrated with CloudWatch, CloudTrail, S3, and Kinesis

  Example Diagram

     Data Sources   Inputs                       Analytics                   Outputs
     ------------   --------                    -------------               ----------
          |             |                             |                          |
          |        |------------------|               |                  |------------------|
          |        | Log Files        |               |                  | Application      |
          |        | Messages         |               |                  |   Monitoring     |
          |        | Metrics          |               |                  |                  |
          |        | Config Info      |         |--------------|         | SIEM             |
          |---->   | Documents        |         |              |         |                  |
          |---->   | Lists            |         | OpenSearch   |         | Searches         |
          |---->   |                  | ------> | Service      | ----->  |                  |
                   | Capture, process |         |              |         | Infrastructure   |
                   | and load data    |         |--------------|         |   Monitoring     |
                   | into OpenSearch  |                                  |                  |
                   |------------------|                                  |Search and analyze|
                                                                         |files, visualize  |
                                                                         |logs on Dashboards|
                                                                         |and get real-time |
                                                                         |insights          |
                                                                         |------------------|

      SIEM (Security Information and Event Management)
        - SIEM applictions help organizations detect, analyze, and respond to security threats
        - security software that gives organizations are bird's-eye-view of activity across their
          entire network so they can respond to threats faster
          - SIEM examples: Splunk, ArcSight EMS (Enterprise Security Management), AT&T Cypersecurity,
          Fortinet, IBM QRadar, McAfee SIEM

  Exam Tips

     Amazon Opensearch
       Loves logs
         - for scenarios that talk about creating a logging solution involving visualization of log
           file analyztics or BI reports, think OpenSearch
        Managed analytics and visualiztion service
        ElasticSearch is predecessor to OpenSearch

14.9 Big Data Exam Tips

     Amazon Redshift FAQs
       https://aws.amazon.com/redshift/faqs/
     Amazon EMR (Elastic MapReduce) FAQs
       https://aws.amazon.com/emr/faqs/
     Amazon Kinesis
       https://aws.amazon.com/kinesis/
     Amazon QuickSight FAQs
       https://aws.amazon.com/quicksight/resources/faqs/
     Amazon Glue
       https://aws.amazon.com/glue/
     Amazon OpenSearch (ElasticSearch) FAQs
       https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/faqs/

    4 Questions to Ask in the Exam
      1. what kind of database works for this scenario?
         - is it a transactionl database that needs to be relational database (RDS) or non-relational database (DynamoDB)
      2. How much data do we have?
         know size limits: Kinesis data payload of a record: 1MB      S3 Object size limit: 5TB
      3. Is serverless a requirement
      4. How do we optimize cost

  What to know for the Exam about Big Data

     Redshift
       Overview
         - a fully managed, petabyte-scale data warehouse service in the cloud
         - it's a very large relational database traditionally used in big data applications
       Relational Database
         - redshift is a relational database
         - it's not a replacement for RDS in traditional applications
       Redshift supports single-AZ and multi-AZ (2-AZ) deployments but only in preview mode (11/30/22)
         - Note: previously supported on 1 AZ deployments

     EMR (Elastic MapReduce)
       Overview
         - is a managed big data platform that allows you to process vast amounts of data using open-source
           tools, such as Spark, Hive, HBase, Flink, Hudi, and Presto
         - it is a open-source managed ETL tool
         - it's AWS's ETL tool
         - EMR is used to transform (processs) and move data
         Note: EMR does offer a serverless option
       EC2 Instances
         - EMR is a managed fleet of EC2 instances running open-source tools for
            ETL (Extract/Transform/load) workloads
          - similar to other open-source managed architecture in that it gets the
            fleet up and running quickly, but the technology isn't proprietary to AWS
         EC2 rules apply
           - you can use Reserved Instances (RI) and spot instance to reduce you costs

     Kinesis
       Real-Time response
         - kinesis is the only service with a real-time response
         - if question asks for a real-time solution to processing or moving data, think Kinesis

       Kinesis vs SQS
         SQS
           - a messaging broker that is simple to use and doesn't require much configuration
           - it doesn't offer real-time message delivery
           - can store messages up to 14 days
         Kinesis
           - more complicated to configure and is mostly used in big data applications
           - it does provide real-time communication
           - it can store data up to a year

     Athena
       Overview
         - interactive query service which enable you to analyze and query data located in S3
           uinsg standard SQL (Structured Query Language)
           - serverless, nothing to provision, pay per query / per TB scanned
           - no need to set up complex Extract/Transform/Load (ETL) processes
           - works directly with data stored in S3
           - essentially, allows you to turn S3 into a database
       Serverless SQL query
         - anytime serverless SQL or query data that stored in S3 comes up, think Athena

     AWS Glue
       Overview
         - serverless ETL service
         - a serverless data integration service that makes it easy to discover, prepare, and combine data
         - It allowss you to perform ETL (Extract/Transform/Load) wokloads without managing underlying servers
         - kinda replacing EMR (Elastic MapReplace)
       Serverless ETL
         - it can help create that schema for you data when paired with Athena

      AWS Glue vs EMR:
        https://www.trianz.com/insights/aws-glue-vs-emr

    Visulization: QuickSight and OpenSearch (ElasticSearch)

      QuickSight
        Oveview
          - a fully managed business Intelligence (BI) data visualization service
          - it allows you to easily create dashboards and share them within your company
          - AWS version of Tableau
        Creating Dashboards
          - QuickSight is your go-to tool for visualizing data
          - does not analyze data, but helps you visualize the data


      OpenSearch (ElasticSearch)
        Overview
          - a managed service allowing you to run search and analytics engine for various use cases
          - it is the successfor to Amazon ElasticSearch service
         logs Analysis
           - opensearch is primarily used for analyzing log files and various documents, especially
             within an ETL process
           - opensearch integrates with CloudWatch Logs so it can easily be used to search them
          ElasticSearch is the predecessor to OpenSearch


         ELK (Elasticsearch [now OpenSearch], Logstash, and Kibana) Stack
             Elasticsearch: Elasticsearch is a distributed search and analytics engine built on Apache Lucene.
                Support for various languages, high performance, and schema-free JSON documents makes Elasticsearch
                an ideal choice for various log analytics and search use cases.
            Logstash: Logstash is an open-source data ingestion tool that allows you to collect data from a variety of
                sources, transform it, and send it to your desired destination.
            Kibana: Kibana is a data visualization and exploration tool used for log and time-series analytics, application
                monitoring, and operational intelligence use cases

        How does the ELK stack work?
         1. Logstash ingests, transforms and sends the data to the right destination.
         2. Elasticsearch indexes, analyzes, and searches the ingested data.
         3. Kibana visualizes the results of the analysis.

         What are AWS offerings for the ELK stack?
            Amazon OpenSearch Service offers the latest versions of OpenSearch, support for 19 versions of Elasticsearch
            (1.5 to 7.10 versions), and visualization capabilities powered by OpenSearch Dashboards and Kibana (1.5 to 7.10
            versions). The service integrates with Logstash as well as other AWS services such as Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose,
            Amazon CloudWatch Logs, and AWS IoT to give you the flexibility to select the data ingestion tool that meets
            your use case requirements.

    Data Pipeline Exam tips
      Overview:
       - a managed Extract, Transform, Load (ETL) service for automating movement and transformation
         of your data
      ETL service
        - a managed ETL service
        - implement automated workflows for movement and transformation of your data
      Integration
        - integrates with storage services (e.g. RDS and S3) and compute services (e.g. EC2 and EMR)
      Data Driven
        - data-driven and task dependent ETL workflows are a perfect use case
          - can require tasks to be dependent on successfully completed tasks
        - define data-driven workflows
        - steps are dependent on previous tasks completing successfully

    Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (MSK)
      Overview
        - AWS managed service for building and running Apache Kafka streaming applications
      Kafka Control Plane operations
        - service handles control-plan operations (Creation, updating, and deletions of clusters)
      Kafka Data Plane operations
        - you manage data plane operations
        - allows you to focus on workloads instead of infrastructure overhead
        - leverage Kafka data-plane operations for producing and consuming streaming data
      logs
        - can push broker logs to CloudWatch, S3, or Kinesis Data Firehose
      API logging
        - API calls are logged to CloudTrail

Question 3n
Which AWS service offers a fully managed way of running search and analytics engines?
Choices:
- Amazon Elastic Analytics Service
- Amazon QuickSight
- Amazon OpenSearch Service
- AWS Athena

Answer: Amazon OpenSearch Service
Amazon OpenSearch Service (the Amazon Elasticsearch Service successor) is a managed service allowing you to run
search and analytics engines for various use cases.

Question 3
_____ allows you to transform data using SQL as it's being passed through Kinesis.
choices:
  - Redshift
  - Kinesis Data Analytics
  - RDS
  - DynamoDB

Answer:
  Kinesis Data Analytics allows you to transform data using SQL. Reference Documentation: Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics


Question 5:
True or False? Redshift supports multi-AZ deployments as a general availability feature for non RA3 clusters.
Choices:
 - True
  - False

Info:
Redshift supports multi-AZ deployments as a preview feature for RA3 clusters. For the purpose of the exam, RedShift does not support multi-AZ deployments.
(https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2022/11/amazon-redshift-multi-az-preview-ra3-clusters/)

Answer
Redshift does NOT support multi-AZ deployments as a general availability feature. AWS does support multi-AZ for RedShift as a preview feature for RA3 clusters. For the purpose of the exam, RedShift does not support multi-AZ deployments.
(https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2022/11/amazon-redshift-multi-az-preview-ra3-clusters/)

Chapter 15 Serverless Architecture

l5.1 Serverless Overview

  History of Computing
    Physical Data Centers
    Virtualization
      - we started to virtualize computers
      - computers running more computers inside them
    The cloud
      - so now we manage virtual compute
    Serverless
      - the focus on code and leave the management of compute architecture behind

  Serverless Benefits
    Ease of Use
      - just bring your code to run, and AWS handles almost everything else
    Event Based
      - serverless compute resources can be brought online in response to an event
    Billing Model
      - "Pay as yo go"
      - only pay for your provisioned resources and the length of runtime

  What's Coming Up
    2 biggest serverless compute in AWS:
      Lambda
        - write your code, build your function, and run it
      Fargate
        - run containers in Fargate
        - No servers, no patching, no mess

  Exam Tips
     Serverless
       - focus on answers that move away from unmanged architecture like EC2
       - favor applications that use Lambda or containers rather than tradional operating systems

15.2 Computing with Lambda

   Example Lambda Function
     https://acloudguru-content-attachment-production.s3-accelerate.amazonaws.com/1624902841354-1043%20-%20S15-L02%20Computing%20with%20Lambda.txt

  What is Lambda
   - serverless compute service that lets you run code without provisioning or managing the underlying server

  Building a Lambda Function
    Runtimes
      - need to use an available runtime or bring your own custom runtime
      - runtimes: JAVA (8, 11), Node.js (12, 14, 15, 18), python (3.7, 3.8, 3.9), .NET CORE (3.1),
        .NET (5, 6), Go (1.x), Ruby (2.7*), custom runtime (on AL2 & AL)
    Permissions
      - if your Lambda function needs to make an AWS API call, you'll need to attach a role
    Networking
      - you can (optionally) define the VPC, subnet, and security groups your functions are a part of
    Resources
      - defining the amount of available memory will allocate how much CPU and RAM your code gets
      - your are billed on the amount of resources you are assigned and length of time to run your lambda function
    Triggers
      - what's going to alert your lambda function to start?
      - defining a trigger will kick Lambda off if that event occurs

  Console Demo
     -> need to create role: instance-stopper 1st
          -> IAM -> Role ->  Create role -> Name: instance-stopper -> Add Permissions ->
             Policies: AWSLambdaExecute, AmazonEC2FullAccess
              -> Create role

    -> Compute -> Lambda -> create function:
       "author from scratch", function name: instance-stopper, runtime: python: 2.7 (no longer supported - used 3.7)
        Execution Role: instance-stopper
          -> Create

          -> Select function ->  add provided function code -> Deploy
          -> Selection function -> add trigger -> trigger configuration: EventBridge (CloudWatch Events),
             Role: Create a new rule -> Rule Name: start-ec2-instance,
             Rule Type: Event Pattern / EC2 / EC2 instance state-Change notification
             -> Add (trigger)


         -> EC2 -> launch instance -> defaults but no name tag -> in demo, Lambda stopped it,
            but provided code does not work with python 3.7

          -> Lambda -> select function -> monitor tab -> show it was invocated 2 times
                                          configuration tab -> configuration
                                           -> can set memory: 128MB to 10240 MB
                                                      ephemeral storage : 512MB to 10240 MB
                                                      timeout: defaults: 3 sec, upto 15 min


          # note above create empty Lambda Function

     Lambda function code used to check if a new EC2 instances has a Name tag, if not, shut it down  :
	#!/usr/bin/python
	import boto3
	import logging

	#setup simple logging for INFO
	logger = logging.getLogger()
	logger.setLevel(logging.INFO)

	#define the connection
	ec2 = boto3.resource('ec2')

	def lambda_handler(event, context):
	    # Use the filter() method of the instances collection to retrieve
	    # all running EC2 instances.
	    filters = [{
	            'Name': 'tag:Name',
	            'Values': ['']
	        },
	        {
	            'Name': 'instance-state-name',
	            'Values': ['running']
	        }
	    ]

	    #filter the instances
	    instances = ec2.instances.filter(Filters=filters)

	    #locate all running instances
	    RunningInstances = [instance.id for instance in instances]

	    #print the instances for logging purposes
	    #print RunningInstances

	    #make sure there are actually instances to shut down.
	    if len(RunningInstances) > 0:
	        #perform the shutdown
	        shuttingDown = ec2.instances.filter(InstanceIds=RunningInstances).stop()
	        print shuttingDown
	    else:
	        print "Nothing to see here"


  Exam Tips

     Lambda
       - if you need to automatically remove entries from a security group, start and stop instances,
         or do anything else that isn't build, think Lambda
       - Lambda is used to add features to AWS
       Limitations
         - know the limitations and general use cases for the service
           max timeout: 15 min, max memory: 10GB
       How does it start - triggers
         - know common triggers - can kickoff from:
             S3 events or cloudWatch (EventBridge) events, API Gateway events, ELB events
       Microservices
         - Lambda excels at running small and lightweight functions
       AWS Ecosystem
         - Lambda plays a major role in the AWS ecosystem
         - it is easy to integrate with many services
       Networking
         - Lambda can run inside or outside a VPC

15.3 Leveraging the AWS Serverless Application Repository

  What is AWS Serverless Application Repository
    Serverless Apps
      - allows user to easily find, deploy, or even publish their own serverless applications
        within their own AWS account
    Sharing Serverless Apps
      - ability to privately share applications within orgs or publicly for the world
    Manifest
      - upload your application code and a manifest file
      - Known as the AWS SAM (Serverless Application Model) template
    Integrations
      - Deeply integrated with the AWS Lambda service
      - Appears with the Console
         -> AWS Console -> Compute -> Serverless Application Repository

  Publish and Deploy in AWS Serverless Application Repository
    Publish
      - publishing apps make them available for others to find and deploy
      - Defined with AWS SAM (Serverless Application Model) templates
        - essentially, cloudFormation templates
      - when you 1st set to publish your app, it is set to private by default (only available to the AWS account)
      - to make public or share with specific accounts, you must explicitly share it
    Deploy
      - find and deploy published applications
      - can browse public apps without needing an AWS account
      - with an AWS account, you can browse with the AWS Lambda Console
      - be careful of trusting all applications - some may have issues or vulnerabilities

  Exam Tips
    AWS Serverless Application Repository
      Templates
        - define whole applications via AWS SAM (Serverless Application Model - CloudFormation) templates
        - private by default
      Publish or Deploy
        - publish your own applications
        - deploy publicly available applications or shared with your account
      AWS Lambda
        - heavily integrated with AWS Lambda

15.4 Container Overview

   What is a Container
     - a standard unit of software that packages up code and all of its dependencies, so the
       application runs quickly and reliably from one computing environment to another

   Container vs VM diagram:

     |------------|------------|
     |Application |Application |
     |------------|------------|      |---------|----------------|
     |Lib/Config  |Lib/Config  |      |  App    |     App        |
     |------------|------------|      |---------|----------------|
     |            |            |      |  Libs   |  Libs |  Libs  |
     | Guest OS   | Guest OS   |      |---------|----------------|
     |            |            |      |Container|    Container   |
     |-------------------------|      |---------|----------------|
     |       Hypervisor        |      |         OS               |
     |-------------------------|      |--------------------------|
     |        Hardware         |      |      Hardware            |
     |-------------------------|      |--------------------------|

           Virtual Machine                  Containers

     Containers only included the required libraries to run you application


   Container Terminology:
     Dockerfile:
       - text document that contains all the commands or instructions that be used to build an image
     Image:
       - immutable file that contains the code, libraries, dependencies, and configuration files
         needed to run an application
     Registry:
       - stores docker images for distribution
       - they can be both private and public
     Container:
       - a running copy of the image that has been created

   Console Demo
      Dockerfile:
         FROM centos:7

         # Install dependencies
         RUN yum update -y
         RUN yum install httpd -y

         # install App1 ('code' is a local directory)
         RUN rm -rf /var/www/html/*
         ADD code /var/www/html
         RUN -sf /dev/stdout /var/log/httpd/access_log
         RUN -sf /dev/stderr /var/log/httpd/error_log

         EXPOSE 80

         # running apache
         CMD ["/usr/sbin/httpd", "-D", "FOREGROUND"]


       # build docker image
       sudo docker build  .
       # reports docker imageID on "Successfully build <imageID>" line

       # verify no website is currently running
       curl localhost

       # run docker image
       sudo docker run -d -p 80:80 <imageID>


       # verify website is now running
       curl localhost

   Exam Tips

      Containers
        - generally more flexible
        - easier to run on-site and move around to different enviroments

        High Level
          - you won't have to dive into the specifics of a Dockerfile
          - why containers: for portability, ease of use, flexibility, run on premise, run on AWS
        Ease of use
          - containers help you easily migrate from on-premises to AWS
        Dev vs Prod
          Dev is prod, and prod is dev - that is a good thing with containers
        High Level

15.5 Running Containers in ECS or EKS

   Amazon Elastic Container Service Anywhere is now generally Available
     https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2021/05/amazon-elastic-container-service-anywhere-is-now-generally-available/


  Containers diagram:
     |---------|----------------|
     |  App    |     App        |
     |---------|----------------|
     |  Libs   |  Libs |  Libs  |
     |---------|----------------|
     |Container|    Container   |
     |---------|----------------|
     |         OS               |
     |--------------------------|
     |      Hardware            |
     |--------------------------|

  Problems with Containers
    - how to handle a fleet of containers?

  Why Elastic Container Service (ECS)
    Management of Containers at Scale
      - ECS can manage 1, 10, hundreds, or 1000's of containers
      - it will appropriately place the containers and keep them online
      - Note: ECS is a AWS proprietary enviroment
    ELB Integration
      - containers are appropriately registered with the load balancer as they come online
        and go offline
    Role Integration
      - Containers can have individual roles attached to them, making security a breeze
    Ease of Use
      - Easy to set up and scale to handle any workload

  Using Kubernetes
     - Kubernetes is an open source management and orchestration platform
     - open-source alternative
     - can be used on-premises and in the cloud
     - AWS-managed version is called Elastic Kubernetes Services (EKS)

  ECS vs EKS (Elastic Kubernete Service)
    ECS
      - proprietary AWS Container management solution
      - Best used when you're all in on AWS and looking for something simple
      - does not work on-premises
        Note: 'ECS Anywhere' does work on-premise

    EKS
      - AWS managed version of open-source Kubernetes container management solution
      - best used when you're not all in on AWS
      - more work to configure and integrate with AWS


  Console Demo
     Note: pre-demo, a image to be deployed was stored in the AWS Repo inside
           ECR (Elastic Container Registry)

    # create ECS task with all the setting required for your container
    -> AWS Console -> Containers -> ECS -> task definition -> create new task definition
        EC2 -> Task Definition Name: task1, container-1: Name: wordpress, Image URI: repository-url/image:tag,
        Task memory: 32M, task CPU: 1, Port Mapping: 80:80
        -> create
    # Create Cluster (note: nothing will be running on it)
    -> AWS Console -> Containers -> ECS -> Custers -> create cluster ->
     Cluster Name: wordpressCluster, VPC: , subnet,
     Infrastucture: EC2, Instance type, scaling
     -> create

     -> select cluster -> run new task -> select task -> deploy

     -> EC2 -> select "ECS instance" -> Public IP Address ->
     web brower -> public IP Address -> see website


  Exam Tips

     ECS
       - if you see containers mentioned, think ECS
       - exception: if open source or on-premises solution, think EKS

     ECS or EKS
       Preference
         - generally, favor using AWS designed services over 3rd party
       Open Source
         - exception: if open source or on-premises solution, think EKS
       High Level
         - ECS and EKS generally handle the running and placement of your containers
       Length
         - great for one-off or long-running applications

15.6 Removing Servers with Fargate

  Problems with Servers
    - scaling containers
    - scaling ec2 resources running the containers

  What is Fargate
    - a serverless compute engine for containers that work with Elastic Container Service (ECS)
      and Elastic Kubernete Service (EKS)
    - AWS owns and manages infrasture
    - requires use of ECS or EKS

  EC2 vs Fargate
    EC2
      - you are responsible for underlying OS
      - ECS pricing model  (can use reserve instances [RI] and spot instances)
      - good for long-running applications
      - multiple containers can share the same host
    Fargate
      - no OS access
      - pay based on resources allocated and run time
      - excel for short-running tasks
      - isolated environments

  How does Lambda Compare to Fargate
    Fargate
      - select fargate when you have more consistent workloads
      - allows docker use across the organization and greater level of control by developers
      - no run time limit
    Lambda
      - select Lambda for unpredictable or inconsistent workloads
      - perfect for applications that can be expressed as a single function


  Console Demo

     Note: pre-demo, a image to be deployed was stored in the AWS Repo inside
           ECR (Elastic Container Registry)

    # create ECS task with all the setting required for your container
    -> AWS Console -> Containers -> ECS -> task definition -> create new task definition
        EC2 -> Task Definition Name: task1, container-1: Name: wordpress, Image URI: repository-url/image:tag,
        Task memory: 12M, task CPU: 0.25, Port Mapping: 80:80
        -> create
    # Create Cluster (note: nothing will be running on it)
    -> AWS Console -> Containers -> ECS -> Custers -> create cluster ->
     Cluster Name: wordpressCluster, VPC: , subnet,
     Infrastucture: Fargate, Instance type, scaling
     -> create

     -> select cluster -> run new task -> select task -> deploy

     -> ECS -> select cluster -> networking -> select Public IP
     web brower -> public IP Address -> see website


  Exam Tips

      Lambda vs Fargate vs EC2
        Lambda
          - use for light-weight functions that run quickly and need to be easily integrated
            into AWS architecture (processing content out of S3, responding to event, API gateway)
        Fargate
          - for containers that do not need to run all the time
        ECS with EC2
          - for containers that need to run all the time and concerned about cost

     Fargate
        Serverless
          Container Serverless compute option
         Containers only
          - ECS or EKS is required
         Fargate vs EC2
           - Fargate is more expensive, but easier to use
         Fargate vs Lambda
           - fargate is for containers and applications that need to run longer
           - lambda excels at short and simple functions
