15.7 Amazon EventBridge (CloudWatch Events)

  What is EventBridge (predecessor known as CloudWatch Events)
    - a serverless event bus
    - it allows you to pass events from a source to an endpoint
    - Essentially, its the glue that holds your severless application together

  Create a Rule
    1. Define a Pattern
      - do you want the rule to be invoked based on an event happening or a schedule
    2. Select Event Bus
      - it this going to be an AWS-based event, custom event, or a partner event?
    3. Select your Target
      - what happens when this event kicks off? Do you trigger a Lambda function,
        post to an SQS queue, send an email?
    4. Tag
      - you need to tag everything
    5. Sit Back
      - wait for event to happen and verify it is working correctly

  Console Demo
    # note based off previous lamda function but changed to start function
    -> AWS console -> Application Integration -> EventBridge -> EventBridge Rule ->
      Name: ebinstStopper -> Next
       ->  Service Provider: AWS, Service Name: EC2, Event Type: EC2 instance state-change notification,
          Specific State(s): stopped -> Next
          target 1: AWS Service, Select a Target: Lambda Function, Function: <LambdaFunctionName>
          -> Next -> Tag: <tagName> -> create


     -> EC2 -> stop instance -> verify it started it

  Exam Tips

     EventBridge
       - if you want to trigger an action based on something that happened in AWS, think EventBridge
       - Any API call that happens in AWS can alert a lambda function, or a variety of
       API Calls
         - very common use case is triggering Lambda functions when an AWS API call happens
       High Level
         - there is more to EventBridge than rules, but it won't be on the exam
       New vs Old
         - EventBridge is the new name, but you might see CloudWatch Events on the exam
       Speed
         - this is the fastest way to respond to things happening in your environment
         - could feed your cloudTrail logs through cloudWatch logs and setup and event
           pattern based on an API call, but that will be slow

---------------------------------------------------
15.8 Storing Custom Docker Images in Amazon Elastic Container Registry (ECR)

  ECR (Elastic Container Registry) Service Overview
    Registry
      - AWS-managed container image registry that offers secure, scalable, and reliable infrastructure
    Private
      - private container image repository with resource-based permissions via IAM
    Supported Form
      - support Open Container Iniative (OCI) images, Docker Images, and  OCI artifacts
    Registry

  ECR components to Know
    Registry
      - a private registry is provided to each AWS account
      - it is regional, and you create one or more for image storage
    Authorization Token
      - Authentication token required for pushing and pulling images to and from registry
    Repository:
      - contains all of your Docker Images, OCI Images, and OCI artifacts
    Repository Policy
      - Control all access to repos and images on a per repository bases
    Images
      - container images that get pushed to and pulled from your repository

  Amzaon ECR Public
    - a similar service for public image repositories

  ECR Features to know
    Lifecycle policies
      - help management of images in your repository
      - define rules for cleaning up unused images
      - ability to test your rules before applying them
    Image Scanning
      - helps identify software vulnerabilities in your container images
      - repositories can be set up to scan on push [image to a repo]
      - retrieve results of scans for each image
    Sharing
      - cross-region support
      - cross-account support
      - configured per repository and per region
        Note: each registry is regional for each account
    Cache Rules
      - pull through cache rules allow for caching public repos privately
      - ECR periodically reaches out to public repo and check current caching status for
        your private version [makes sure your private version is up to date with public version]
    Tag Mutability
      - option to prevent image tags from being overwritten
      - configured on per repository level

    Note: Amazon ECR offers the ability to enable scan on push, which enables software vulnerability scanning of
          all images pushed to your repositories.
          Reference: What is Amazon Elastic Container Registry?
            https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECR/latest/userguide/what-is-ecr.html

  ECR Integrations
    Bring Your Own
      - you are able to leverage ECR images within your own  container infrastructure
      - can use ECR images with on-premise container infrastructure
    Amazon ECS
      - use ECR container images in ECS container definitions
    Amazon EKS
      - use [pull] ECR container images for your EKS clusters
    Amazon Linux
      - Amazon Linux containers can be used locally for your software development

  Exam Tips
     Elastic Container Repository (ECR)
       Container Image storage
         - AWS-managed container image registry service
       Supported Formats
         - supports Open Container Iniative (OCI) images, Docker Images, and  OCI-compatible artifacts
       Lifecycle Policies
         - rules defining when to expire and remove unused or older images
       Image Scanning
         - helps identify software vulnerabilities in your container images
         - scan on push to repository settings allows for identifying software vulnerabilities in
           your container images
       Tag Mutability
         - option helps prevent image tags from being overwritten
       Keywords to look for
          - questions related to a managed container image registry, OCI repositories, or image
            integration with ECS and EKS

15.9 Using Open-Source Kubernetes in Amazon EKS Distro

  Amazon EKS Distro (EKS-D) Overview
    EKS-D
      https://distro.eks.amazonaws.com/
      - a kubernete distribution based on and used by Amazon EKS to create reliable and
        secure Kubernetes clusters
    Similarities
      - it has the same versions and dependencies deployed by Amazon EKS
    Differences
      - EKS-D is fully managed by you unlike EKS, which is managed by AWS
    Where
      - run EKS-D anywhere - on-premises, in the cloud, etc.
    Your Responsibility
      - you are fully responsible for upgrading and managing your platforms

  Exam Tips and Important Takeways

   Elastic Kubernete Serice Distro (EKS-D):
     Keywords
       - look out for questions related to self-managed Kubernete deployments that
         are similar to Amazon EKS
     Uses
       - Use EKS-D when you need to run versioned deployments of clusters outside of
         AWS managed services
     Not common on Exam
       - it does not commonly appear on exams and functions like EKS Anywhere

    Note:
        Amazon EKS Anywhere provides an installable software package for creating
        and operating on-premises Kubernetes clusters that are based on Amazon EKS Distro,
        the same Kubernetes distribution used by Amazon EKS for clusters on AWS

15.10 Orchestrating Containers Outside AWS using Amazon EKS Anywhere and Amazon ECS Anywhere

  EKS Anywhere Overview
    On-Premises EKS
      - an on-premises way to manage Kubernete (K8s) clusters with the same practices
        used by Amazon EKS
    Based on EKS Distro
       - allows for deployment usage, and management methods for clusters in your data centers
    Lifecycle
      - offers full lifecycle management of multiple K8s clusters
      - operates independently of AWS

  EKS Anywhere Concepts
    Control Plane
      - K8s control plane management is operated completely by the customer
    Control Plane
      - K8s control plane location is entirely within a customer data center or
        operation center
    Updates
      - cluster updates are done entirely via manual CLI or Flux
      Note: flux is a command line utility for assembling Kubernetes CD (continuous
            deployment?) pipelines
    Curated Packages
      - curated packages offer extended core functionalities of K8s clusters
    Enterprise Subscriptions
      - Curated packages requires an AWS Enterprise subscription

  ECS Anywhere Overview
    ECS Anywhere
      - feature of Amazon ECS allowing the management of container-based apps on-premises
    No Orchestration Needed
      - no need to install and operate local container orchestration software, meaning
        more operation efficiency
    Completely Managed
      - completely managed solution enabling standardization of container management
        across environments
      - leveraging ECS standards, practices, and protocols on-premises
    Inbound Traffic?
      - Keep in mind, no ELB support make inbound traffic requirements less efficient
    External
      - new launch type noted as EXTERNAL for creating services or running tasks

  ECS Anywhere Requirments
    1. Must have the SSM Agent, ECS agent, and Docker installed
    2. You must first register external instances as SSM Managed Instances
    3. Easily create an installation script within the ECS console to prepare
       you external instances and servers
    4. Installation script contain SSM activation keys and commands for the
       required software
    5. Execute installation scripts on your on-premise VMs or bare-metal servers
    6. Deploy containers using EXTERNAL launch type

  ECS Anywhere Example Diagram

     On premises Data Center                AWS
           |--------------|          |----------------|
           | Install:     |          |                |
           | ECS Agent    | -------> |  Amazon ECS    |
           | SSM Agent    | ----|    |                |
      VM --| Docker       |     |--> |  AWS Systems   |
           |              |          |   Manager      |
           |              |          |                |
           | Start:       |          |----------------|
           | Containers   |
           |--------------|

  Exam Tips

    EKS Anywhere
      Not Common on Exam
        - not commonly featured on Exam
      Anywhere
        - Based on the EKS Distro
        - it allows you to run EKS K8s clusters on-premises
      AWS Efficiency
        - Distro allows customers to maintain similar operational efficiency to Amazon EKS
      Managed by you
        - everything is managed by you - including control plan management and location
    ECS Anywhere
      Amazon ECS
        - a feature with the ECS service itself
      Anywhere
        - it allows for AWS-managed container orchestration anywhere on-premises
      Requirements
        - You must have SSM Agent, ECS agent, and Docker installed and configured
      Register and Deploy
        - Execute Installation Scripts containing required steps, and use the EXTERNAL
          launch type

15.11 Auto Scaling Databases on Demand with Amazon Aurora Serverless

  Aurora Serverless Overview
    Understand the difference between:
        Aurora Provisioned (standard Aurora) vs Aurora Serverless
    Aurora Serverless
      On Demand
        - on-demand and  auto scaling configuration for the Amazon Aurora database service
      Automated
        -  automation of monitoring workloads and adjusting capacity for databases
      Based on Demand
        - Capacity adjusted based on application demands
      Billing
        - charged only for resources consumed by DB clusters; per-second billing
      Budget Friendly
        - helps customers stay well within budgets via the auto scaling and per-second
          billing features

  Aurora Severless Concepts
    Aurora Capacity Units (ACUs):
      - measurements of how your clusters scale
    Min and Max ACUs
      - you set a minimum and maximum of ACUs for scaling requirements - can be zero (shutdown)
    AWS-managed warm pools
      - ACUs are allocated quickly by AWS-managed warm pools
    Each ACU
      - combination of 2 GB of memory, matching CPU, and network capability
    Data resiliceny
      - as data resilicency as Aurora Provisioned - 6 copies of data across 3 AZs
    Highly Available option
      - use multi-AZ deployments for establishing highly available custers

  Aurora Severless Popular Use Cases
    Variable Workloads
      - unpredicatable or sudden activity workloads
    Multi-Tenant Apps
      - let the service manage database capacity fore individual app
    New Apps
      - unsure what database instance needs are required
    Dev and Test
      - development and testing of new features
    Mixed-Use Apps
      - app might server more than one purpose with different traffic spikes
      - e.g. online transaction processing with different spikes in traffic
    Capacity Planning
      - easily swap from provisioned to serverless or vice versa

  Exam Tips
    Aurora Serverless
      On Demand
        - on-demand, auto scaling version of Amazon Aurora database service
        - capacity adjusted for you
    Aurora Capacity Units (ACUs):
      - measurements of how your clusters scale
      - set a minimum and maximum of ACUs for scaling requirements - can be zero (shutdown)
      - billed per second only for used resources
     Popular Exam Scenarios
       - variable workloads, new applications, capacity planning, and development and testing needs
    Data resiliceny
      - as data resilicency as Aurora Provisioned - 6 copies of data across 3 AZs
    Multi-AZ option for Highly Availability
      - use multi-AZ deployments for highly available workloads

15.12 Using AWS X-Ray for Application Insights

  AWS X-Ray Concepts
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/xray/latest/devguide/xray-concepts.html

   AWS X-Ray service Overview
     App Insights
       - X-Ray collects application data for viewing, filtering, and gaining insights
         about requests and responses
     Downstream
       - view calls to downstream AWS resources and other micorservices/APIs or databases
     Traces
       - X-Ray recevives traces from your applications for allowing insights
     Multiple Options
       - integrated services can add tracing headers, send trace data, or run the
         X-Ray daemon (e.g. on a EC2 instance)
          - e.g. Lambda can send trace data about your Lambda functions

   AWS X-Ray Exam Concepts
     Segments
       - data containing resource names, request details, and other information
     SubSegments
       - segments providing more granular timing information and details
     Service Graph
       - graphical respresentation of interacting services in requests
     Traces
       - trace ID track paths of requests and traces collect all segments in a request
     Tracing Header
       - Extra HTTP header containing sampling decisions and trace ID
       - traces are configured for a certain minimum. After the minimum is reached, only
         a percentage of requests are traced to minimize costs
         of the
     X-Amzn-Trace-Id
       - name of tracing header containing added information

   AWS X-Ray Daemon
     - AWS software application that listens on UDP port 2000
     - it collects raw segment data and sends it to the AWS X-Ray APP
     - When the daemon is running, it works along with the AWS X-Ray SDKs

   X-Ray AWS Integrations
     Amazon EC2
       - install run X-Ray Agent on EC2 Application servers so you can gather insights
     Amazon ECS
       - install with ECS tasks
     Amazon Lambda
       - simple on/off toggle and is built-in and available for Lambda functions
     AWS Elastic Beanstalk
       - Configuration option
     Amazon API Gateway
       - added to each stage as desired
     Amazon SNS and SQS
       - view time taken for messages in queues and topics

   Exam Tips and Important Takeaways

     AWS X-Ray Exam Tips
       App Insights
         - X-Ray collects application data for viewing, filtering, and gaining insights
           about requests and responses
       Integrations
         - many AWS integrations available such as EC2 (run as a daemon), Lambda (toggle on/off),
           and API Gateway (can be added to each stage)
       Important Terms
         - traces, tracing headers, and segments
       Exam Keywords
         - Scenarios involving app requests insights, viewing response times of downstream
           resources, and HTTP response analysis

---------------------------------------------------
15.13 Deploying GraphQL Interfaces in AWS AppSync

  What is AWS AppSync
    - Robust, scalable GraphQL interface for application developers
    - Combines data from multiple sources (e.g. DynamoDb and Lambda)
    - enable data interaction for developers via GraphQL
    - GraphQL: data language that enables apps to fetch data from servers
    - Seamless integration with React, React Native, iOS, and Android, & JavaScript

  What is GraphQL
    - GraphQL is a data language to enable client apps to fetch, change and subscribe to
      data from servers.
    - In a GraphQL query, the client specifies how the data is to be structured when it is
      returned by the server. This makes it possible for the client to query only for the data
      it needs, in the format that it needs it in.

  Exam Tips and Important Takeways

    AWS AppSync
      - Service for scalable GraphQL interfaces for application developers
    Keywords
      - GraphQL, fetching app data, declarative coding, and frontend app data fetching

15.14 Serverless Architecture Exam Tips


  4 Questions to Ask yourself in the Exam
    1. is the application right for containers or should it be hosted on something like ec2?
    2. Do you need those servers?
    3. Is the application AWS specific?
    4. How long does your code need to run?

  AWS Lambda
    Lambda loves IAM roles:
      - whenever you are talking about credentials and lambda, ensure you are attaching
        a role to the lambda function
    Lambda triggers:
      - commonly see questions asking what can kick off a Lambda function.
      - Know that S3, Kinesis, and EventBridge (former CloudWatch Events) are common triggers
    Lambda Limitations:
      - lambda functions should be short
      - you can allocate up to 10 GB of RAM and 15 minutes of runtime
    Any AWS API Call:
      - AWS API call can kickoff an EventBridge rule to execute an AWS Lambda function which could
        then call an EventBridge rule
      - AWS API call can be trigger to kick off an EventBridge rule
      - EventBridge rule is faster that trying to scrape through CloudTrail

  Containers and Images
    Open source = Kubernete
      - for a container management solution that can run in AWS and on-premise, you will
        want to consider EKS or EKS Anywhere
    Fargate can't work alone
      - In order to use Fargate, you must be using ECS or EKS
    Containers offer flexibility
      - container can encompass just about any workload
      - Generally, it preferred to use containers rather than EC2 on the exam
    Know the Basics
      - know you start with a Dockerfile, build an image, upload that image to a repo,
        and then run it on a host
    AWS-managed image registry
      - AWS Service needed for image storage or image vulnerability scanning, think of ECR

  Amazon Aurora Serverless
    On-demand or autoscaling database
       - questions regarding auto scaling databases likely involve Aurora Serverless databases
    Variable traffic or workloads
      - if designing a new application with unknown workloads or traffic spikes to the database,
        look for answers including this service
    Capacity Planning
      - for capacity planning for a new application with a database or a developement environment,
        think Aurora Serverless

  AWS X-Ray
    App Insight
      - X-Ray is nded to gain appliciation insights using requests and responses of services
        and functions at different points in the application flow
    Traces and downstream response times
      - if these terms are brought up in the questions, think AWS X-Ray
    Lambda or API Gateway insights
      - X-Ray integrates natively with Lambda and API Gateway and can easily helpe gain
        deeper insights and understand of your workload requests and responses

  AWS AppSync
    Managed GraphQL interface
      - for a managed GraphQL interface for any frontend application or developer will likely
        include AWS AppSync

15.15 Triggering AWS Lambda from Amazon SQS

  Learning objectives
   - Create the Lambda Function
   - Create the SQS Trigger
   - Copy the Source Code into the Lambda Function
   - Log In to the EC2 Instance and Test the Script
   - Confirm Messages Were Inserted into the DynamoDB Table


lambda function code:
from datetime import datetime
import json
import os
import boto3

dynamodb = boto3.resource('dynamodb')

def lambda_handler(event, context):
    # Count items in the Lambda event
    no_messages = str(len(event['Records']))
    print("Found " +no_messages +" messages to process.")

    for message in event['Records']:

        print(message)

        # Write message to DynamoDB
        table = dynamodb.Table('Message')

        response = table.put_item(
            Item={
                'MessageId': message['messageId'],
                'Body': message['body'],
                'Timestamp': datetime.now().isoformat()
            }
        )
        print("Wrote message to DynamoDB:", json.dumps(response))


$ cat send_message.py
#!/usr/bin/env python3.7
# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
import argparse
import logging
import sys
from time import sleep
import boto3
from faker import Faker


parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()
parser.add_argument("--queue-name", "-q", required=True,
                    help="SQS queue name")
parser.add_argument("--interval", "-i", required=True,
                    help="timer interval", type=float)
parser.add_argument("--message", "-m", help="message to send")
parser.add_argument("--log", "-l", default="INFO",
                    help="logging level")
args = parser.parse_args()

if args.log:
    logging.basicConfig(
        format='[%(levelname)s] %(message)s', level=args.log)

else:
    parser.print_help(sys.stderr)

sqs = boto3.client('sqs')

response = sqs.get_queue_url(QueueName=args.queue_name)

queue_url = response['QueueUrl']

logging.info(queue_url)

while True:
    message = args.message
    if not args.message:
        fake = Faker()
        message = fake.text()

    logging.info('Sending message: ' + message)

    response = sqs.send_message(
        QueueUrl=queue_url, MessageBody=message)

    logging.info('MessageId: ' + response['MessageId'])
    sleep(args.interval)



  Solution

  Log in to the AWS Management Console using the credentials provided on the lab instructions page. Make sure you're using the us-east-1 (N. Virginia) region.
  Create the Lambda Function

      In the search bar on top, enter lambda.
      From the search results, select Lambda.
      Click the Create function button.
      On the Create function page, select Author from scratch.
      Under Basic Information, set the following parameters for each field:

      Function name: Enter SQSDynamoDB.
      Runtime: Select Python 3.9 from the dropdown menu.
      Architecture: Select x86_64.

      Under Permissions, expand Change default execution role.
      Select Use an existing role.
      Under Existing role, select lambda-execution-role from the dropdown menu.
      Click the Create function button.

  Create the SQS Trigger

      Click the + Add trigger button.
      Under Trigger configuration, click the Select a source dropdown menu.
      From the menu, select SQS.
      Under SQS queue, click the search bar and select Messages.
      Ensure that the checkbox next to Activate trigger is checked.
      Click Add.

  Copy the Source Code into the Lambda Function

      Under the + Add trigger button, click the Code tab.
      On the left side, double-click on lambda_function.py.
      Delete the contents of the function.
      Navigate to the link below to the source code for lambda_function.py:

  https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ACloudGuru-Resources/SQSLambdaTriggers/master/lambda_function.py. 6. Copy the code. 7. Return to the AWS console and paste the code into the lambda_function.py code box. 8. Click the Deploy button.
  Log In to the EC2 Instance and Test the Script

      In the search bar on top of the AWS console, enter sqs.

      From the search results, select Simple Queue Service.

      Click Messages.

      Click the Monitoring tab to monitor our SQS messages.

      In the search bar on top, enter ec2.

      From the search results, select EC2 and open it in a new browser tab or window.

      Under Resources, click Instances (running).

      In the existing instance available, click the checkbox next to its name.

      Click the Connect button at the top.

      Click Connect at the bottom to open a shell and access the command line.

      In the shell, become the cloud_user role:
      su - cloud_user

      View a list of files available to you:
      ls

      View the contents of the send_message.py file:
      cat send_message.py

      Start sending messages to our DynamoDB table from our Messages SQS queue with an interval of 0.1 seconds:
      ./send_message.py -q Messages -i 0.1

      After a few seconds, hit Control + C to stop the command from continuing to run.

  Confirm Messages Were Inserted into the DynamoDB Table

      Return to the browser tab or window with the Messages queue in Amazon SQS open. You may have to wait a few minutes to see results showing up in the tables, but you should soon see a spike in the table Number of Messages Received.
      In the search bar on top, enter dynamodb.
      From the search results, select DynamoDB.
      In the left-hand navigation menu, select Tables.
      Select the Message table.
      In the top-right corner of the page, click Explore table items and review the list of items that were inserted from our script, sent to SQS, triggered Lambda, and inserted into the DynamoDB database.

15.16 AWS Certified Solutinon Architect - Associate (SAA-C03) Serverless Architecture

Question 4:
You need to manage a container-based application on-premises via an AWS service. Which of the following would meet your needs?
Choices:
 - Amazon EKS Anywhere
 - Amazon ECS Anywhere
 - AWS Lambda containers
 - Amazon EKS

While [EKS Anywhere] this allows you to run Kubernetes clusters on-premises using the EKS Distro,
this is not managed via AWS. It is entirely self-managed.

Correct Answer
With Amazon ECS Anywhere, you can run and manage container-based applications on-premises, including
on your own virtual machines and bare metal servers.

Chapter 16 Security

---------------------------------------------------
16.1 DDoS Overview
  What is DDoS (Distributed Denial of Service) Attack
    - DDoS attack is an attack that attempts to make website or application unavailable to
      you end users
    - This can be acheived by multile mechanisms, such as large packet floods, by using a
      combination of reflection and amplification techniques, or by using large botnets

  Layer 4 DDoS Attacks
    - a layer 4 DDoS attack is often referred to as SYN flood
    - it works at the transport layer (TCP)
    - to establish a TCP connection a 3-way handshake takes place
      - the client sends a SYN packet to a server, the server replies with SYN-ACK, and
        the client then responds to that with an ACK
    What Should Happen:
      - after the "3-way handshake" is completed, the TCP connection is established.
      - After this the application begin sending data using Layer 7 (application layer
        protocol), such as HTTP, etc
    SYN Floods
      - A SYN flood uses the built in patience of the TCP stack to overwhelm a server by
        sending a large number SYN packets and then ignoring the SYN-ACKs returned by the server
      - this causes the server to use up resources waiting for a set amount of time for the
        anticipated ACK that should come for a legitimate client
    What Can Happen:
      - There a only so many concurrent TCP connections that a web or application server
        can have open, so if an attacker sends enougth SYN packets to a server, it can
        easily eat through the allowed number of TCP connections
      - this then prevents legitimate requests from being answered by the server

  Amplification Attacks
    - Amplification/reflection attacks can include things such as NTP (Network Time Protocol:
      used to sync system clocks), SSDP, DNS, CharGEN, SNMP attacks, etc.
    - This is where an attacker may send a third-party server (such as an NTP server) a
      request using spoofed IP Address [the spoofed address is the server being attacked]
    Amplification Attacks
      - that [NTP] server will then respond to that request with a greater payload than the initial
        request (usually with the region of 28 - 54 times larger than the request) to the
        spoofed IP address [IP address being attacked]
      - this means that if the attacker sends a packet with a spoofed IP address of 64 bytes,
        the NTP server would respond with 3456 bytes of traffic to the spoofed IP address

  Layer 7 Attacks
    - A layer 7 attack occurs where a web server receives a flood of GET [page] or POST requests,
      using from a botnet or a large number of compromised computers

       Attacker ---> sends flood of GET Requests ---->| Web Server
                                                      |
                              End User ---XXXXXX----> |
                              - requests fail because attacker overwhelmed Web Server resources

  Exam Tips

      DDoS attack
        - a Distributed Denila of Service (DDoS) attack attempt to make your website or
          application unavailable to your end users
        - Common DDoS attacks include layer 4 attacks such as "SYN Floods" or "NTP amplification"
          attacks
        - Common Layer 7 DDoS attacks include floods of "GET/POST requests"

16.2 Logging API Calls with CloudTrail


  AWS CloudTrail FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/faqs/

  Validating CloudTrail log file integrity
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awscloudtrail/latest/userguide/cloudtrail-log-file-validation-intro.html

  What is CloudTrail
     - increases visibility into your user and resource activity by recording AWS Management
       Console actions and API calls
     - You can identify which users and accounts called AWS, the source IP address from which
       the calls were made, and when the calls occurred

  CloudTrail Network Diagram

                           Control

      AWS User   Console  |Public  |    |AWS Cloud          |
                 ------>  | API    |    |  DynamoDB         |

                     logged in S3 CloudTrail Bucker

       - metadata around API calls

       - the time of the API call
       - the source IP address of the API caller
       - the request parameters
       - the response elements returned by the service
    What CloudTrail Allows?

      - near real-time intrustion detection
        - can integrate cloudTrail with Lambda functions to implement intrusion detection

16.3 Protecting Applications With Shield

  AWS Shield FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/shield/faqs/

  AWS Shield Features > Specialized support
    https://aws.amazon.com/shield/features/#Specialized_support

  What is AWS Shield?
    - Free DDoS (Distributed Denial Of Service) Protection
    - protects all AWS customers on Elastic Load Balancers (ELB), CloudFront, and Route 53
    - protects against SYN/UDP Floods, reflection attacks and other Layer 3 and Layer 4 attacks

  What is Shield Advanced
     - provides enhanced protections for your applications running on protected EC2, ELB, CloudFront,
       AWS Global Accelerator, and Route 53 against larger and more sophisticated attacks
     - offers always-on, flow-based monitoring of network traffic and active application montoring
       to provide near real-time notifications of DDoS attacks
     - gives you 24/7 access to the DDoS Response Team (DRT) to help manage and mitigate application-layer
       DDoS attacks
     - protects your AWS bill against higher fees due to ELB, CloudFront, and Route 53 usage spikes
       during a DDoS attack

  What are the Shield Costs
     Shield Standard: Free
     Shield Advanced: $3K / month

  Console Demo
     # enable Shield Advanced:
     -> AWS Console -> Security, Identity & compliance -> WAF & Shield -> (left side) -> AWS Shield
       -> Subscript to Shield Advanced -> click the 4 boxes -> enable

  Exam Tips

    AWS Shield
       - Shield (standard) protects only against Layer 3 and Layer 4 DDoS attacks on your ELB, CloudFront,
         and Route 53 (according to Shield FAQs, Shield protects applications running on AWS, not just these services)
    AWS Shield Advanced:
      - $3K/month, but will give a dedicated 24/7 DDoS Response Team
      - provides enhanced protections for your applications running on project EC2, ELB, CloudFront, and Route 53
        against larger and more sophisticated attacks

    AWS WAF (Web Application Firewall)
       - for application level (layer 7) attacks, WAF is used to protect against

16.4 Filtering Traffic with AWS WAF

   What is AWS WAF
     - a Web Application Firewall (WAF) that lets you monitor HTTP and HTTPS requests that are forwarded
       to your CloudFront or Application Load Balancer
     - WAF also lets you control access to your content
     - you can configure conditions such as what IP addresses are allowed to make this request or what query
       string parameters need to be passed for the request to be allowed
     - The Application Load Balancer, CloudFront, or API Gateway will either allow this content to be received or give an
       HTTP 403 status code

   AWS WAF operates at Layer 7

   At its most basic level, AWS WAF allows 3 different Behaviors
     1. Allow all requests except the ones your specify
     2. Bock all requests except the ones your specify
     3. Count the  requests that match the properties that you specify

   Conditions
     You can define conditions by using characteristics of web requests such as the following
       1. IP Addresses that the requests orginate from
       2. Country that the request originate from
       3. Values in the request headers
       4. Presence of SQL code that is likely to be malicious (known as SQL injection)
       5. Presence of a script that is likely to be malicious (known as cross-site scripting)
       6. Strings that appear in requests - either specific strings or strings that match regular
          expression (regex) patterns

   Exam Tips

      AWS WAF
        - WAF operates at Layer 7
        - WAF can block Layer 7 DDoS attacks as well as things like SQL injections and cross-site scripting
        - WAF can block access from specific countries or IP Addresses
        - Create a rate-based rule on your AWS WAF and associate the web access control list (ACL) to
          the Application Load Balancer

16.5 Guarding your Network with GuardDuty

  What is GuardDuty
    - a threat detection service that uses machine learning to continuously monitor for malicious
      behavior
    - looks for:
      -  unusual API calls, calls from known malicious IP addresses
      - attempts to disable CloudTrail logging
      - unauthorized deployments
      - compromised instances
      - reconnaissance by would-be-attackers
      - port scanning, failed logins

  GuardDuty Features
    - alerts appear in GuardDuty console and CloudWatch Events
    - Receive feeds from 3rd parties like Proofpoint and ClowdStrike, as well as AWS Security, about
      known malicious domains and IP addresses, etc.
    - Monitors CloudTrail Logs, VPC Flow Logs, and DNS Logs
    - Centralize threat detection across multiple AWS accounts
    - Automated response using CloudWatch Events and Lambda
    - Machine Learning and anomaly detection

  Setting Up GuardDuty
     GuardDuty
       - is threat detection with AI
     7 to 14 days to set a baseline
       - what is normal behavior on your account
     Once Active:
       - you will see findings on the GuardDuty console and in CloudWatch Events only if GuardDuty detects
         behavior it considers a threat

  GuardDuty Pricing
    - 30 days free
    - Charges based on:
      - quantity of CloudTrail events
      - Volume of DNS and VPC Flow Logs data

  Exam Tips

     GuardDuty
       - Uses AI to learn what is normal behavior looks like in your account and to alert you any abnormal
         malicious behavior
       - updates a database of known malicious domains using external feeds from 3rd parties
       - monitors your CloudTrail logs, VPC Flow logs, and DNS logs
       - Findings appear in the GuardDuty dashboard. CloudWatch events can be used trigger a
         lambda function to address a threat

16.6 Centralizing WAF (Web Application Firewall) Management via AWS Firewall Manager

  What is a Firewall Manager
    - a security management  service in a single pane of glass
    - this allows you to centrally set up and manage firewall rules across multiple AWS accounts and
      applications in AWS organizations

  Manage Security across Multiple Accounts
    - using Firewall Manager, you can create new AWS WAF rules for your Application ELBs, API Gateways,
      and CloudFront Distributions
    - you can also mitigate DDoS attacks using AWS Shield Advanced for your Application ELB, Elastic IP
      addresses, CloudFront distributions, and more

  Firewall Manager Benefits
     Simplify Management of Firewall Rules across your accounts
       - one single pane of glass allows you to manage security across multiple AWS services and accounts
     Ensure Compliance of Existing and New Applications
       - Firewall Manager automatically enforces security policies that you create across existing and
         newly created resources, across multiple accounts

  Exam Tips

    AWS Firewall Manager
      - if multiple AWS accounts and resources need to be secured centrally, think Firewall Manager

16.7 Monitoring S3 Buckets With Macie

  Personally Identifiable Information (PII)
    - personal data used to establish individual's identity
    - home address, email address, SSN
    - Passport number, driver license number
    - D.O.B, phone number, bank account, credit card number

  What is Macie:
    - uses machine learning and pattern matching to discover sensitive data stored in S3
    - Uses AI to recognize if your S3 objects contain sensitive data such as PII, PHI (Personal
      Health Information), and financial data
    - alerts you to unencrypted buckets
    - alerts you to public buckets
    - can alert you about buckets shared with AWS accounts outside of those identified in your
      AWS organizations
    - Great for frameworks like HIPAA and GDPR
      Note: HIPAA (Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act) - governs how to US store medical records
            GDPR (General Data Protection Regulation): EU PII handling regulation

  Macie Alerts
     - you can filter and search Macie alerts in the AWS console
     - Alerts sent to EventBridge can be integrated with your Security incident and Event Management
       (SIEM) system
     - Can be integrated with AWS Security Hub for a broader analysis of your organization's security posture
     - Can also be integrated with other AWS services, such as Step Functions, to automatically take
       remediation actions

      Note:
      SIEM (Security Information and Event Management)
        - SIEM applictions help organizations detect, analyze, and respond to security threats
        - security software that gives organizations are bird's-eye-view of activity across their
          entire network so they can respond to threats faster
          - SIEM examples: Splunk, ArcSight EMS (Enterprise Security Management), AT&T Cypersecurity,
          Fortinet, IBM QRadar, McAfee SIEM

  Exam Tips

     Macie
       - uses AI to analyze data in S3 and help identify PII, PHI, and financial data
       - great for HIPAA and GDPR compliance as well as preventing identify theft
       - Macie alerts can be sent to EventBridge and integrated with your event management systems
       - automate remediation actions using other AWS services, such as Step Functions

16.8 Securing Operationg Systems with Inspector

  What is Amazon Inspector
    - inspector your network and EC2 instances
    - an automated security assessment service that helps improve the security and compliance
      of applications deployed on AWS
    - automatically assesses applications for vulnerabilitties or deviations from best practices
    - e.g. left a port 22 open on your security group
  Assessment Findings
    - after performing an assessment, Inspectory produces a detailed list of security findings
      prioritized by level of severity
    - these findings can be reviewed directly or as part of a detailed assessment reports that are
      available via Inspector console or API

  2 types of Assessments
    network Assessments
      - Network configuration analysis to check for ports reachable from outside the VPC
      - inspector agent is not required
    Host Assessments
      - Vulnerable software (CVE), host hardening (CIS Benchmarks), and security best practices
      - inspector agent is required
      Notes:
        CIS (Center for Internet Security) benchmarks: a set of globally recognized and consensus-driven
           best practices to help security practitioners implement and manage their cybersecurity defensess
        CVE (Common Vulnerabilites and Exposures):  a glossary that classifies vulnerabilities. The glossary
           analyzes vulnerabilities and then uses the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) to evaluate
           the threat level of a vulnerability

  How does it work
     1. in Inspector dashboard, create assessment target
     2. Install agents on EC2 instances
       - AWS will automatically install the agent on instances that allow Systems Manager Run command
     3. Create assessment template
     4. perform assessment run
     5. review findings against rules

  Exam tips
    AWS Inspector
      - used to perform vulnerability scans on EC2 instances and VPCs
      network Assessments
        - Network configuration analysis to check for ports reachable from outside the VPC
        - inspector agent is not required
      Host Assessments
        - Vulnerable software (CVE), host hardening (CIS Benchmarks), and security best practices
        - inspector agent is required
      Assessments
        - can run assessments once or, alternatively, run them weekly

16.9 Managing Encryption Keys With Key Management Service (KMS) and CloudHSM


   AMS Key Management Service (KMS) FAQs
     https://aws.amazon.com/kms/faqs/

   Deleting KMS Keys
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/deleting-keys.html


  What Is AWS KMS?
    - a managed service that makes it easy for you to create and control encryption keys
      used to encrypt your data
    Integration
      - KMS is integrated with other AWS services such as EBS, S3, RDS - as well as other
        services to make it simple to encyrpt your data with encryption keys you manage
    Controlling your keys
      - KMS provides you with centralized control over the lifecycle and permissions of
        your keys
      - you can create new keys whenever you wish, and you can control who can manage
        keys separately from who can use them
    Customer Master Key (CMK)
     - a CMK is a logical representation of a master key.
     - the CMK includes metadata, such as the key ID, creation date, description, and key state
     -  the CMK also contains the key material used to encrypt and decrypt data
    Getting Started
      - you start using the service by requesting the creation of a CMK
      - you control the lifecycle of the CMK as well as who can use or manage it

  Hardware security Module (HSM)
    - a HSM is a physical computing device that safeguards and manages digital keys
      and performs encryption and decryption functions
    - an HSM encontains one or more secure cryptoprocessor chips

  3 ways to Generate a CMK
    1. AWS creates the CMK for you
      - the key material for a CMK is generated within HSMs managed by AWS KMS
    2. Import Key material from you key management infrastructure and associate it with a CMK
    3. Have the key material generated and used in AWS CloudHSM cluster as part of the
       custom key store feature in AWS KMS

  KMS Key Rotation
    - you can choose to have AWS KMS automatically rotate CMKs every year, provided that those
      keys were generated within AWS KMS HSMs (option 1 - AWS creates the CMK for you)
    - automatic key rotation is not supported for imported keys, asymmetric keys, or keys
      generated in an AWS cloudHSM cluster using AWS KMS custom key store feature

  KMS Policies
    - the primary way to manage access to your KMS CMKs is with policies.
    - Policies are documents that describe who has access to what
    - Policies attached to an IAM identity are called identity based policies, and
      policies attached to other kinds of resources are called resource-based policies
    - in KMS, you must attach resource-based policies to your Customer Master Keys (CMKs).
      These are called Key Policies
    - All KMS CMKs have a key policy

  3 ways to control permissions
    Use a Key Policy
      - controlling access this way means the full scope of access to the CMK is defined
        in a single document (the key policy)
    Use IAM Policies in combination with Key Policy
      - Controlling access this way enables you to manage all the permissions for you IAM
        identity in IAM
    Use grants in combination with Key Policy
     - Controlling access this way enable you to allow access to the CMK in the key policy,
       as well as allow users to delegate their access to others

  What is AWS CloudHSM
    - a cloud-based Hardware Security Module (HSM) that enables you to easily generate
      and use your own encryption keys on the AWS Cloud
    - It is a physical device, entirely dedicated to you, that can be deployed in a highly
      available fashion

  KMS vs CloudHSM
    KMS
      - shared tenancy of underlying hardware
      - automatic key rotation
      - automatic key generation
    CloudHSM
      - dedicated HSM to you
      - full control of underlying hardware
      - full control of users, groups, keys, etc.
      - no automatic key rotation

  Exam Tips

     AWS KMS
       - KMS is managed service that makes it easy for you to create and control encryption keys
         used to encrypt your data
     Getting Started with KMS
      - you start using the service by requesting the creation of a CMK (Customer Master Key)
      - you control the lifecycle of the CMK as well as who can use or manage it

     3 ways to Generate a CMK (Customer Master Key)
       1. AWS creates the CMK for you
         - the key material for a CMK is generated within HSMs (Hardware Security Module) managed by AWS KMS
       2. Import Key material from you key management infrastructure and associate it with a CMK
       3. Have the key material generated and used in AWS CloudHSM cluster as part of the
          custom key store feature in AWS KMS

     3 ways to control permissions
       Use a Key Policy
         - controlling access this way means the full scope of access to the CMK is defined
           in a single document (the key policy)
       Use IAM Policies in combination with Key Policy
         - Controlling access this way enables you to manage all the permissions for you IAM
           identity in IAM
       Use grants in combination with Key Policy
        - Controlling access this way enable you to allow access to the CMK in the key policy,
          as well as allow users to delegate their access to others

     KMS vs CloudHSM
       KMS
         - shared tenancy of underlying hardware
         - automatic key rotation
         - automatic key generation
       CloudHSM
         - dedicated HSM to you
         - full control of underlying hardware
         - full control of users, groups, keys, etc.
         - no automatic key rotation

16.10 Storing Your Secrets in Secrets Manager


  What is Secrets Manager
    - a service that securely stores, encrypts, and rotates your database credentials
      and other secrets
    - encryption in transit and at rest using KMS
    - automatically rotates credentials
    - apply fine-grained access control using IAM policies
    - cost money, but it is highly scalable

  What else can Secrets Manager do:
    - your application makes an API call to Secrets manage to retrieve the secret
      programmatically (instead of hardcoding them in your application)
    - reduces the risk of credentials being compromised

  What you can store in Secrets Manager
    - RDS credentials
    - non-RDS databases credentials
    - any other type of secret, provided you can store it as key-value pair (SSH keys, API keys)

  Automatic Rotation
    - if you enable rotation, secrets manager immediately rotates the secret once
      to test the configuration
    - Ensure all of your applications that use these credentials are updated to retrieve
      the credentials from this secret from Secrets Manager
  Disable Rotation
    - if you applications are still using enbedded credentials, do not enable rotation because
      the enbedded credentials will no longer work and this will break you application
  Enable Rotation
    - this is the recommended setting if your applications are not already using embedded
      credentials (i.e., they are not going to try to connect to the database using the old
      credentials)

  Exam Tips
    Secrets Manager
      - used to securely store your application secrets:
        database credentials, API Keys, SSH keys, passwords, etc
      - Applications use the Secrets Manager API to interact with the Secrets Manager
      Credential Rotation
      - rotating credentials is super easy - but be carefull that your applications
        are not using embedded credentials
      - when enabled, Secrets Manager will rotate credentials immediately
      - make sure all your application instances are configured to use Secrets Manager
        before enabling credential rotation

---------------------------------------------------
16.11 Storing Your Secrets in Parameter Store

  What is Parameter Store
    - a capability of AWS Systems Manager that provides secure, hierarchical storage for
      configuration data management and secrets management
    - you can store data such as passwords, database strings, Amazon Machine Images (AMI) IDs,
      and license codes as parameter values
      - you can store values as plain text of encrypted data

  Parameter Store Costs
    - it is free

  Parameter Store Limitation
    - limit to the number of parameters you can store (currently: 10,000)
    - no key rotation

  Exam Tips
     Parameter Store or Secrets Manager
        For exam, when should use Parameter Store vs Secrets Mnagers?
          - if you are trying to minimize cost, choose Parameter Store
          - if you need more than 10K parameters, key rotation, or ability to generate
            passwords using CloudFormation, use Secrets Manager

---------------------------------------------------
16.12 Temporarily Sharing S3 Objects Using Presigned URLs or Cookies

  Privacy
    All objects in S3 are private by default
      - only the object owner has permission to access these objects
      - however, the object owner can optionally share objects with others by creating
        a presigned URL, using their own security credentials, to grant time-limited
        permission to download the objects
  Presigned URLs
    - when your create a presigned URL for your object, you must provide your security
      credentials, specify a bucket name and object key, and indicate the HTTP method
      (or GET to download the object) as well as expiration date and time
    - presigned URLs are only valid for the specified duration
  Presigned URL Access
    - anyone who receives the presigned URL can then access the object
    - For example, if you have a video in your bucket and both the bucket and the object
      are private, you can share the video with others by generating a presigned URL
    - this is a very popular scenario base exam question

  Presigned Cookies
    - this can be useful when you want to provide access to multiple restricted files
    - the cookie will be saved on the user's computer, and they will be able to browse the
      entire contents` of the restricted content
    - e.g. could use cookie to provide access to entire content of website after they have signed up

  Console Demo
    -> AWS console -> S3 -> Create bucket : Bucket Name: presignedurlbucketphall -> create bucket
    -> select: presignedurlbucketphall bucket -> upload photo
    -> click on object URL -> access denied

    -> log in to EC2 instance with S3 access role:
    # list bucket content - photo name
    $ aws s3 ls s3:<bucketName>
    # generate presigned URL for  photo  (default expires--in is 3600 seconds)
    $ aws s3 presign s3:<bucketName>/<photo>  --expires-in 3600
    # cut and paste generated URL to web browser and photo is displayed

  Exam Tips

    Presigned URLs
      -  if you need to share a private files in your S3 buckets, think presigned URLs
      - presigned URL can be generated via command line (AWS CLI) or programmatically
      - presigned URL expiration time (expires-in) can be set in seconds (default: 3600 sec)

---------------------------------------------------
16.13 Advanced IAM Policy Documents

  Amazon Resource Names (ARNs)
   - uniquely identify a resource in AWS
   - ARNs all begin with:
     arn:partition:service:region:account_id:
     e.g.:
     arn:aws:s3:::pathall1stbucket

     partition: normally "aws", but would be "aws-cn" in china
     service: s3, ec2, rds
     region: us-east1, eu-central-1
     account id:  12 digit account number

   ARNs end with
       resource
       resource_type/resource
       resource_type/resource/qualifier
       resource_type/resource:qualifier
       resource_type:resource
       resource_type:resource:qualifier


    ARNs examples
       arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/ryan
          "::" -> obmitted value - because IAM is global, so it has not region value
       arn:aws:s3::myawsbucket:user/image.png
          ":::" -> obmitted values - S3 is an univeral namespace, so region and account id values are not needed
       arn:aws:dynamodb:us-east-1:123456789012:tables/orders
       arn:aws:ec2:us-east-1:123456789012:instance/*
         "*" wildcard for all ec2 instances in  us-east-1 region

  IAM Policies
    - JSON document that defines permissions
    - Identity Policy: when applying policies to users and groups
    - Resource Policy: when applying policies to AWS resources
    - policies have no effect until they are attached
    - list of statements

    example S3 full access

    {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "s3:*",
                "s3-object-lambda:*"
            ],
            "Resource": "*"
        }
      ]
    }

   example AWSLambdaDynamoDBExecutionRole

   {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "dynamodb:DescribeStream",
                "dynamodb:GetRecords",
                "dynamodb:GetShardIterator",
                "dynamodb:ListStreams",
                "logs:CreateLogGroup",
                "logs:CreateLogStream",
                "logs:PutLogEvents"
            ],
            "Resource": "*"                         # ARN for resource
        }
      ]
    }


    {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",                  # policy version
    "Statement": [
                                              # A policy document is a list of statements:
        {                                     # statement 1
          . . .
        },
        {                                     # statement 2
          . . .
        },
        {                                     # statement 3
          . . .
        },
        {                                     # statement 4
          . . .
        }
      ]
    }


   example from lession:

   {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "SpecificTable",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "dynamodb:BatchGet",
                "dynamodb:DescribeStream",
                "dynamodb:DescribeTable",
                "dynamodb:Get",
                "dynamodb:Query",
                "dynamodb:BatchWrite",
                "dynamodb:CreateTable",
                "dynamodb:Delete",
                "dynamodb:Update",
                "dynamodb:PutItem",
            ],
            "Resource": "arn:aws:dynamodb:*:*:table/MyTable"
        }
      ]
    }

  Note: Sid: (Optional) Include an optional statement ID to differentiate between your statements.

  IAM Policies
    - a policy document is a list of statements
    - each statement matches an AWS API required
    - effect is either to "Allow" or "Deny"
    - matched based on their Action
    - And the resource the action is applied to

  IAM Permission Policies vs IAM Permission Boundary
    IAM Permission Policies
      - set permissions to resources by attaching permission policies to users or group
    IAM Permission Boundary
      - set permissions boundaries by attaching permission boundary policies
      - set a permission boundary to control the maximum permissions for the user
      - use this advanced feature to delegate permission management to other

  IAM Permissions Boundaries
    - used to delegate administration to other users
    - prevent privilege esculation or unnecessarily broad permissions
    - control the maximum permissions an IAM policy can grant

   Permission Boundaries Use Cases
   https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/access_policies_boundaries.html
     - developers creating roles for Lambda functions
     - Application owners creating roles for EC2 instances
     - Admins create ad hoc users

     permission boundaries example:
      - For example, assume that the IAM user named ShirleyRodriguez should be allowed to manage
        only Amazon S3, Amazon CloudWatch, and Amazon EC2. To enforce this rule, you can use the
        following policy to set the permissions boundary for the ShirleyRodriguez user:

     {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "s3:*",
                "cloudwatch:*",
                "ec2:*"
            ],
            "Resource": "*"
        }
      ]
     }

  Exam Tips

   IAM Policy Documents
     - not explicitly allowed == implicitly denied
     - explicit deny > everything else
        e.g. explicit S3 deny access wins over also having an explicit allow S3 access
     - only attached policies have effect
     - AWS joins all applicable policies to a resource
       e.g. have ec2 admin access attached to your developers and S3 access attached to our developer group
     - AWS managed policies vs customer managed policies

  IAM Policies
    - JSON document that defines permissions
    - Identity Policy: when applying policies to users and groups
    - Resource Policy: when applying policies to AWS resources
    - policies have no effect until they are attached
    - list of statements

  Resource-based Policies
    - are attached to a resource.
    - For example, you can attach resource-based policies to Amazon S3 buckets, Amazon SQS queues,
      VPC endpoints, and AWS Key Management Service encryption keys
    - you can specify who has access to the resource and what actions they can perform on it.

  Resource-based Policies vs Resource-level permissions
    - You can attach resource-based policies directly to a resource
    - Resource-based policies are supported only by some AWS services.
    - Resource-level permissions refer to the ability to use ARNs to specify individual resources in a policy

---------------------------------------------------
16.14 AWS Certificate Manager

  What is the AWS Certificate Manager?
    - allows you to create, manage, and deploy public and private SSL certificates for use
      with outher AWS services
    - it integrates with other services - such as Elastic Load Balancing, CloudFront Distribution,
      and API Gateway - allowing you to easily manage and deploy SSL certificates in you AWS environment

  AWS Certificate Manager Benefits
    Cost
      - no more paying for SSL certificates
      - AWS certificate Manager provisions both public and private certificates for free
      - you will still pay for the resources that utilize your certificates (such as ELB)
      - do not need to go to Verisigm, Comodo, etc and pay for certificates
    Automated Renewal and Deployment
      - Certificate Manager can automate the renewal of your SSL certificates and then automatically
        update the new certificates with ACM-integrated services, such as Elastic Load Balancing,
        CloudFront, and API Gateway
    Easier to Set up
      - Removes a lot of the manual process, such as generating a key pair or creating a certificate
        signing request (CSR)
      - you can create your own SSL certificate with just a few clicks in the Console

  Exam Tips
     AWS Certificate Manager
       Know what it is:
        - In the exam, you will get scenario-based questions around SSL certificates
          and what services you should use to integrate SSL certificates
       Supported Services
         - AWS Certificate Manager integrates with Elastic Load Balancer, CloudFront, and API Gateway
       Benefits
         - free servcie that saves time and money
         - automatically renew your SSL certificates and rotate the old certificates with new
           certificates with supported AWS services

16.15 Auditing Continuously with AWS Audit Manager


  What is Audit Manager?
    - with it, you can continually audit your AWS usage to make sure you stay compliant with
      industry standards and regulations
    - Audit Manager is an automated service that produces reports specific to auditors for PCI
      compliance, GDPR, and more
      Notes:
          PCI-DSS (Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard)
          GDPR (General Data Protection Regulation): EU PII handling regulation
          HIPAA (Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act) - governs how to store US medical records

  Audit Manager Use Cases
    Transition from Manual to Automated Evidence Collection
      - it allows you to produce automated reports for auditors and reduces the need to
        compile these reports manually
    Continuous Auditing and Compliance
      - it work on a continuous basis so that, as your AWS environment evolves and adapts,
        you can produce automated reports to evaluate your environment against industry
        standards (such as PCI compliance)
    Internal Risk Assessments
      - you can create a new framework from the beginning or customized prebuild frameworks
      - you can also launch assessments to automatically collect evidence, helping you validate
        if your internal policies are being followed

  Exam Tips
    Continuous Auditing with AWS Audit Manager
      - if you have a scenario question about HIPAA or GDPR compliance that asks about continuous
        auditing or automating auditing reports, think AWS Audit Manager

16.17 Authenticating Access with Amazon Cognito

  What is Cognito
    - authentification agent
    - provides authentication, authorization, and user management for your web and mobile
      apps in a single service without the need for custom code
    - your users can sign in directly with a username and password they create or through
      a 3rd part (e.g. Facebook, Amazon, Google, or Apple)

  Cognito Features
    - Sign-up and sign-in options for you apps
    - Access for guest users
    - Acts as an identity broker between your application and the web ID providers, so
      so you don't need to write an custom code
    - Synchronizes user data across multiple devices
    - recommended for all mobile applications that call AWS services

  Cognito Use Cases
    Authentication
      - users can sign in using a user pool or 3rd party identity provider, such as Facebook
    3rd-party Authentication
      - user can authenticate using identity pools that require an identity provider (IdP) token
    Access Server-side resources
      - a signed-in user is given a token that allows them access to resource that you specify
    Access AWS AppSync Resources
      - user can be given access to AppSync resources with tokens received from a user or identity
        pool in Cognito

  Cognito User Pools and Identity Pools
    - the 2 main components of Cognito are user pools and identity pools
    user pools
      - directories of users that provide sign-up and sign-in options for your application users
    identity pools
      - allow you to give your users access to AWS services
      - you can use identity pools and user pools either separately or together

  How Cognito works broadly

Note: STS: AWS Security Token Service

  Device        Login Provider       Amazon Cognito         AWS STS
  -------           --------              -------          ---------
     |----- Login ----->|                    |                  |
     |<-----------------|                    |                  |
     |                  |                    |                  |
     |----- GetId ------|------------------->|                  |
     |                  | <---validation---->|                  |
     |<-----------------|------------------->|                  |
     |                  |                    |                  |
     |                  |                    |                  |
     | --GetOpenIDToken-|------------------->|                  |
     |                  | <---validation---->|                  |
     |<-----------------|------------------->|                  |
     |                  |                    |                  |
     |------------------|-AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity------------>|
     |                  |                    |<--validation---->|
     |<-----------------|--------------------|----------------->|
     |                  |                    |                  |


  Cognito Sequence

                        |-------------------|
              |<----------> user pool       |     1. Authentication and get token
       device |         |                   |
              |<----------> identity pool   |     2. Exchange token and get AWS Credentials
              |         |                   |
              |<----------> AWS Services    |     3. Access AWS services using credentials
                        |    s3   dynamoDB  |
                        |                   |
                        |-------------------|

  Exam Tips

    Cognito Exam Tips
      user pools
        - directories of users that provide sign-up and sign-in options for users of your application
      identity pools
        - allow your users to access to other AWS services
      user pools + identity pools
      - you can use identity pools and user pools either separately or together


     Cognito Sequence

                        |-------------------|
              |<----------> user pool       |     1. Authentication and get token
       device |         |                   |
              |<----------> identity pool   |     2. Exchange token and get AWS Credentials
              |         |                   |
              |<----------> AWS Services    |     3. Access AWS services using credentials
                        |    s3   dynamoDB  |
                        |                   |

16.18 Analyzing Root Cause Using Detective

  What is Detective?
    - using detective, you can analyze, investigate, and quickly identify the root
      causes of potential security issues or suspicious activties
    - Detective pulls data in your from your AWS resources and uses machine learning,
      statistical analysis, and graph theory to build a linked set of data that enables
      you to quickly figure out the root cause of your security issues

  Detective Sources
    - detective uses a number of sources within your AWS account (including VPC Flow Logs,
      CloudTrail Logs, Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service audit logs, and Amazon GuardDuty
      findings) to automatically create an overview of your users, resources, and
      interactions between them over time

  Detective Use Cases
    Triage security Findings
      - if you have suspected security instance, you need to be able to quickly assess if it's
        in fact real or false positive
      - Detective generates visualizations that can show you what resources, IP addresses, and
        AWS accounts are connected to your security incident to determine if the finds is
        actual malicious activity or not
    Threat Hunting
      - in comparison to root cause analysis, you can also be proactive and go threat hunting
      - detective helps with threat hunting by creating detailed visualization on specific
        resources such as IP addresses, AWS accounts, VPC, and EC2 instances

  Exam Tips

    Amazon Detective
      - using detective, you can analyze, investigate, and quickly identify the root
        causes of potential security issues or suspicious activties
      - Detective pulls data in your from your AWS resources and uses machine learning,
        statistical analysis, and graph theory to build a linked set of data that enables
        you to quickly figure out the root cause of your security issues
      - operates across multiple AWS services and analyzes the "root cause" of an event
      vs Inspector
      - do not confuse with Inspector, which is automated vulnerability management
        service that continually scans EC2 and container workloads for software
        vulnerabilities and unintended network exposure


---------------------------------------------------
16.19 Protecting VPCs with AWS Network Firewall

   AWS Network Firewall FAQs
      https://aws.amazon.com/network-firewall/faqs/

   What is an AWS Network Firewall?
     - physical firewall to your VPC
     - a managed service that makes it easy to deploy physical firewall protection across your VPCs
     - it has managed infrastructure (i.e., a physical firewall managed by AWS)

     - Network firewall includes a firewall rule engine that gives you complete control over your
       network traffic, allowing you to do things such as block outbound Server Message Block (SMB)
       requests to the spread of malicious activity

   Network Firewall Benefits
     - has physical infrastructure in the AWS datacent that is managed by AWS staff, so you don't need
       to worry about the management overhead
     - AWS Network Firewall works with AWS Firewall Manager, so you can centrally manage security
       policies across existing and newly created accounts and VPCs
     - It also provides intrusion prevention system (IPS) that gives you active traffic flow inspection

   Network Firewall Use Cases
     Filter Internet Traffic
       - you can use methods like access control list (ACL)  rules, stateful inspection,
         protocol detection, and intrusion prevention to filter your internet traffic
     Filter Outbound Traffic
       - you can use network firewall to provide the URL/domain name, IP address, and content-based
         outbound traffic filtering
       - This will help you to stop possible data loss and block known malware communications
     Inspect VPC-to-VPC traffic
       - you can automatically inspect traffic moving from one VPC to another as well as across
         multiple accounts

   Exm Tips
     AWS Network Firewall
       - physical firewall to your VPC
       - a managed service that makes it easy to deploy physical firewall protection across your VPCs
       - it has managed infrastructure (i.e., a physical firewall managed by AWS)
       - questions aboutn filtering your network traffic before it reaches your internet gateway,
         or if you require intrusion prevention systems or any hardwary firewall requirements,
         think Network Firewall
       - OSI layer 3 - 7 protection, statefull & stateless, large IP block/allow lists, etc

16.20 Leveraging AWS Security Hub for Collectiong Security Data

  What is Security Hub?
    - a single place to view all your security alerts from services like GuardDuty,
      Amazon Inspector, Amazon Macie, and AWS Firewall Manager
    - it works across multiple accounts

  Security Hub Use Cases
    Conduct Cloud Security Posture Management (CSPM)
      - use automated checks that comply with common frameworks (such as CIS or PCI-DSS) to
        help reduce your risks

        note:
        CIS (Center for Internet Security) benchmarks: a set of globally recognized and consensus-driven
           best practices to help security practitioners implement and manage their cybersecurity defensess
    Correlate Security Findings to Discover New Insights
      - aggregate all your security findings in one place allowing your security staff to more
        easily identify threats and alerts

  Exam Tips
     Security Hub
       - question about a single place to view all your security alerts across multiple AWS
         security services and accounts, think Security Hub
